# International Organizations and Decolonization in the Twentieth Century: From the League of Nations to the United Nations and Beyond
# 1 Introduction: Framing the Paradox of International Organizations and Decolonization

The twentieth century witnessed two seemingly contradictory transformations unfold in tandem: the consolidation of formal international organization as a defining feature of modern world politics, and the dismantling of the European colonial empires that had structured the global order for centuries. These were not parallel developments running on separate tracks. Rather, the League of Nations, the United Nations, and their associated specialized agencies and committees became the very institutional terrain on which the legitimacy, contestation, and ultimate undoing of colonial rule were negotiated. To understand how empire ended — and how it did not — one must examine the international institutions through which it was simultaneously preserved, supervised, criticized, and overthrown. Conversely, to understand the trajectory of twentieth-century international organization — its expanding membership, its shifting normative vocabulary, its evolving agenda — one must reckon with how decolonization reshaped it from within.

This report takes as its central problem the paradoxical relationship between these two processes. It proceeds from the conviction that neither a narrative of progressive emancipation, in which international institutions gradually fulfilled their universalist promises by midwifing self-determination, nor a narrative of unrelieved imperial complicity, in which such institutions merely refashioned colonial domination into more diffuse forms, can capture the actual historical record. The relationship between international organizations and decolonization was rather one of mutual constitution: each was repeatedly transformed by its entanglement with the other, often in ways unintended by their architects and contested by their participants.

## 1.1 Scope, Periodization, and Core Concepts

The analytical scope of this report extends from the founding of the League of Nations in 1919 — and more specifically from the inscription of Article 22 of the Covenant, which established the Mandates System — through the post-Charter era of the United Nations and into the late twentieth century, when the formal phase of decolonization had largely run its course but its institutional legacies continued to shape global governance. The report concludes its main analytical arc with the rise and reversal of the New International Economic Order (NIEO) agenda in the 1970s and early 1980s, while acknowledging in the closing chapter that the unfinished business of decolonization extends into the contemporary period.

Three periodizations organize the analysis, each corresponding to a distinct institutional and political configuration. The **interwar period (1919–1945)** is treated as the era in which the principle of international supervision over colonial territories was first institutionalized, however imperfectly, through the League's Mandates System, and in which the first systematic transnational anti-colonial counter-mobilization — embodied paradigmatically by the League Against Imperialism founded in Brussels in 1927 — sought to construct an alternative international politics from outside official institutions. The **post-1945 UN era (1945–1960)** marks the codification of decolonization norms in the UN Charter, the transformation of mandates into trusteeships under Chapters XII and XIII, the elaboration of obligations toward non-self-governing territories under Chapter XI, and the gradual conversion of the General Assembly from a forum of cautious consultation into a primary arena of anti-colonial diplomacy. The **post-1960 era of structural transformation** opens with General Assembly Resolution 1514 (XV), the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples, and traces the reshaping of the UN by the influx of newly independent states, the emergence of the Non-Aligned Movement and the Group of 77, the articulation of the NIEO, and the development of new operational functions including peacekeeping, development assistance, and human rights monitoring.

Several core concepts require disciplined definition at the outset, since their meanings shifted significantly across the period under study. **Decolonization** is here understood not as a discrete legal event — the lowering of one flag and the raising of another — but as a long, uneven, and contested process encompassing the formal transfer of sovereignty, the construction of new state institutions, the renegotiation of economic relations, and the contestation of inherited cultural and intellectual hierarchies. **International organization** is used in the institutional sense to refer to inter-state bodies endowed with permanent secretariats, formal membership, and delineated functional competences, but the report also attends to the transnational, non-state networks (anti-imperialist leagues, solidarity movements, expert communities) that operated alongside and against official institutions. **Mandate** and **trusteeship** are treated as historically specific legal categories rather than interchangeable terms: the mandate was a creation of the League Covenant predicated on the formula of the "sacred trust of civilization" and classified into A, B, and C categories reflecting graduated assumptions about readiness for self-government; trusteeship, codified in the UN Charter, retained much of the supervisory architecture of its predecessor but introduced an explicit telos of self-government or independence and accommodated voluntary placement of additional territories.

**Self-determination**, perhaps the most contested concept of all, undergoes a remarkable trajectory in the period under study, moving from a Wilsonian and Leninist rhetorical principle of the post-1918 settlement, applied principally to European nationalities, to a circumscribed political norm in the early UN years, and ultimately to a near-universal quasi-legal right anchored in Resolution 1514 (1960), the 1966 International Covenants on Human Rights, and subsequent General Assembly practice. **Sovereignty**, similarly, mutates from a formal-juridical attribute of European states, conditionally extended to others through doctrines of "civilization" and "standards," into a principle whose substantive content — territorial integrity, control over natural resources, non-intervention — becomes a central demand of newly decolonized states. The report attends carefully to these conceptual mutations, treating them not as background assumptions but as objects of analysis in their own right.

The choice of an institutional focus centered on the League–UN continuum requires brief justification. Other framings are possible: one might privilege bilateral relations between metropoles and colonies, regional organizations such as the Organisation of African Unity or the Arab League, or the transnational circuits of anti-colonial activism. This report acknowledges all of these and integrates them where relevant, but it argues that the League–UN axis provides a uniquely productive analytical frame because these were the institutions in which the legitimacy of empire was formally adjudicated, in which the legal vocabulary of self-determination was authoritatively elaborated, and in which the demographic and ideological transformations wrought by decolonization were most directly registered. They were, in a non-trivial sense, the sites at which the global order itself was constituted and reconstituted.

## 1.2 The Central Paradox: Guardians of Empire and Arenas of Anti-Colonial Struggle

The report is organized around a single guiding paradox: international organizations of the twentieth century were, simultaneously and not merely sequentially, instruments of imperial continuity and platforms of anti-colonial contestation. This is not a paradox to be resolved by demonstrating that one face of the institution was "really" primary while the other was epiphenomenal; it is a structural feature of these institutions that must be held in view throughout the analysis.

On the side of imperial continuity, the evidence is substantial. The League of Nations Mandates System emerged from the wartime allocation of former Ottoman and German territories among the victorious powers, and the mandates were administered, in practice, very much as colonies — by Britain in Palestine, Iraq, and Tanganyika; by France in Syria and Lebanon and parts of Cameroon and Togo; by Belgium in Ruanda-Urundi; by South Africa in South West Africa; by Australia, New Zealand, and Japan in various Pacific territories. The Permanent Mandates Commission, though it received reports and petitions, had no executive authority to compel changes in colonial administration. At the founding of the United Nations in 1945, the colonial powers retained extensive control over the design of the Charter's provisions on dependent territories; Chapter XI's Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories was deliberately framed in less binding terms than the trusteeship provisions, and the determination of which territories qualified as "non-self-governing" was initially left to administering powers themselves. The five permanent members of the Security Council, with their veto power, included four colonial or formerly colonial powers, and this structural inequality has endured to the present.

Yet the same institutions, often in the same instruments, opened spaces that anti-colonial actors used to remarkable effect. Article 22 of the Covenant, however much it codified a hierarchy of "civilizations," also introduced the principle of non-annexation and the formula of the "sacred trust," which colonized petitioners would invoke against their mandatory powers. The Permanent Mandates Commission's petition procedure was strategically appropriated by indigenous nationalists, missionary networks, humanitarian advocates, and minority groups, who used it to publicize grievances on an international stage that did not previously exist. The UN Charter's Article 73, despite its limitations, generated reporting obligations whose interpretation became a site of sustained anti-colonial pressure in the Fourth Committee and the Committee on Information from Non-Self-Governing Territories. The General Assembly, where each member state had one vote and where colonial powers could be outvoted by an emerging Afro-Asian coalition, became by the late 1950s and 1960s the primary diplomatic theater of decolonization. Resolution 1514 of 1960, adopted with no negative votes and only nine abstentions (notably the colonial powers and their close allies), transformed self-determination from a contested political principle into something approaching a peremptory norm of international law.

The duality is thus not an accidental by-product but a constitutive feature of these institutions. It manifests across virtually every domain the report examines: in the mandates regime, where the language of trusteeship simultaneously legitimized continued European rule and provided a vocabulary for its critique; in trusteeship petitions, where colonized peoples spoke directly to a body of international officials; in plebiscites in Togoland, the Cameroons, and elsewhere, which translated abstract principles of consent into concrete electoral processes; in General Assembly debates, where the rhetorical commitments of colonial powers could be turned against them; and in the specialized agencies, which gradually shifted from instruments of metropolitan technical expertise into channels of development assistance and capacity-building. Throughout the report, this paradox functions less as a thesis to be defended than as an analytical injunction: to attend, in every case, both to the structural constraints inherited from empire and to the institutional openings through which those constraints were challenged.

## 1.3 Historiographical Approach and Interpretive Framework

This report is written at a moment when the historiography of international organizations and decolonization has been substantially reconfigured by several waves of revisionist scholarship. An earlier institutionalist literature, often produced by international lawyers and political scientists associated with the United Nations system, tended to read the trajectory from League to UN as a story of progressive normative achievement, in which the principle of self-determination matured from a Wilsonian aspiration into a binding legal norm and through which the international community gradually shed its imperial inheritance. A subsequent body of critical and post-colonial scholarship subjected this teleology to searching critique, demonstrating the deep continuities between imperial governance and international administration, the persistence of racialized hierarchies in technical assistance and development discourse, and the constraints that the structures of the UN — its veto, its weighted financial arrangements, its Eurocentric procedural cultures — imposed on the substantive claims of newly independent states. More recent global, transnational, and Global South–centered historiographies have moved beyond this opposition, recovering the agency of colonized actors within international institutions, tracing transnational anti-colonial networks across the official–unofficial divide, and exploring the unintended consequences of imperial designs.

This report situates itself within this third historiographical moment without claiming to settle the debates it engages. Its interpretive stance can be summarized in four commitments. First, it refuses teleology in both its progressive and its declinist variants. The trajectory from 1919 to the late twentieth century was not the predetermined unfolding of a universal norm of self-determination, nor was it a closed circuit in which imperial domination reproduced itself in ever more sophisticated forms; it was a contingent process whose outcomes were repeatedly open and contested. Second, it insists on the agency of colonized actors as participants in, not merely objects of, international politics — whether as petitioners to the Mandates Commission, as anti-imperialist organizers in Brussels in 1927, as delegates at Bandung in 1955, or as architects of the NIEO declarations of 1974. Third, it treats the institutional and the ideational as mutually constitutive: voting procedures, committee structures, and reporting obligations shaped what could be said and demanded, while shifting normative discourses (sacred trust, self-determination, sovereign equality, economic justice) reshaped what institutions could do. Fourth, it distinguishes consistently between normative and material achievements: the codification of a principle in a resolution is not equivalent to its implementation, and the report makes a point of identifying cases where rhetorical victories coexisted with material defeats — as, paradigmatically, in the trajectory of the NIEO.

The cast of actors whose interactions structure the analysis is correspondingly broad. Imperial powers — Britain, France, Belgium, the Netherlands, Portugal, and the settler states of South Africa, Australia, and New Zealand, alongside Japan and the United States in distinctive roles — appear throughout, but they are not the sole protagonists. Anti-colonial leaders and movements — among them Jawaharlal Nehru, Mohammad Hatta, Kwame Nkrumah, Gamal Abdel Nasser, Sukarno, and the broader networks of the League Against Imperialism, the Bandung Conference, and the Non-Aligned Movement — are central. International bureaucrats and experts, from the secretariat of the League's Mandates Section to UN officials such as Ralph Bunche and Dag Hammarskjöld, played roles that were neither neutral nor straightforwardly imperial. Transnational non-state actors — missionary networks, humanitarian organizations, anti-slavery societies, and later NGOs concerned with apartheid and racial discrimination — operated across the boundary between official institutions and civil society. The two Cold War superpowers, the United States and the Soviet Union, pursued strategies of competitive engagement with the Third World that both constrained and enabled anti-colonial projects. And the developing nations themselves, whose internal heterogeneity is often understated, organized themselves through coalitions — the Afro-Asian group, the NAM, the G77 — whose coordination and internal tensions are essential to the story.

This multi-actor, multi-scalar approach has methodological implications. It requires reading official documents (treaties, resolutions, Charter provisions) alongside the strategies of those who deployed them; it requires attending to what was achieved, what was demanded but not achieved, and what was excluded from articulation altogether; and it requires resisting the temptation to render the history neat. The report accordingly accepts a certain degree of analytical complexity as a virtue rather than a defect.

## 1.4 Report Structure and Analytical Roadmap

The chapters that follow are organized to trace the central paradox across the three periodizations outlined above, while developing thematic arguments that cumulate toward an integrated interpretation.

The following table summarizes the architecture of the report and indicates how each chapter contributes to elucidating the central paradox.

| Chapter | Period / Theme | Contribution to the Central Paradox |
|---|---|---|
| 2. The Mandates System | Interwar institutionalization | Shows how Article 22 simultaneously legitimized continued European rule and inaugurated unprecedented international scrutiny |
| 3. The League Against Imperialism | Interwar counter-mobilization | Examines transnational anti-colonial politics that operated against, but in dialogue with, official international institutions |
| 4. From Mandates to Trusteeship | Transition 1945 | Analyzes continuities and ruptures in the UN's codification of decolonization norms |
| 5. The General Assembly as Arena | Late 1940s–1950s | Traces the conversion of UN forums into anti-colonial battlegrounds through procedural and coalitional politics |
| 6. Resolution 1514 (1960) | The watershed | Reconstructs the normative transformation of self-determination into a quasi-legal right |
| 7. Reshaping the Organization | 1955–1970s | Quantifies and interprets the demographic and agenda-setting transformation of the UN, and the East–West to North–South shift |
| 8. NAM, G77, and the NIEO | Third World diplomacy | Examines the high point and reversal of the Global South's economic-justice agenda |
| 9. New Functions | Peacekeeping, development, human rights | Explores how decolonization generated new operational roles, themselves shaped by colonial politics |
| 10. Conclusion | Synthesis | Reassesses the entangled legacies and unfinished agendas |

Chapter 2 examines the League of Nations Mandates System, analyzing the legal foundations of Article 22, the political compromises that produced the A/B/C classification, and the work of the Permanent Mandates Commission and its petition procedures. It argues that the Mandates System constituted a genuine innovation — the first systematic international supervision of colonial governance — while also operating, in practice, largely as an instrument of continued imperial administration; and it traces how this ambiguity was exploited by colonized petitioners to internationalize their grievances.

Chapter 3 turns to the most significant interwar counter-institution: the League Against Imperialism, founded at the Brussels Congress of February 1927. It reconstructs the organization's ideological foundations at the intersection of Comintern networks and nationalist movements, the participation of figures including Nehru, Hatta, and delegates from across Asia, Africa, and Latin America, its principal campaigns, its internal fractures between communist and non-communist anti-imperialists, and its longer-term legacy for the post-1945 Third World project.

Chapter 4 analyzes the transition from mandates to trusteeship, comparing the legal architectures of the two regimes, examining wartime debates among the Great Powers over the future of empire, and assessing the significance of Chapter XI's Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories as a broader if weaker normative innovation than the trusteeship provisions themselves.

Chapter 5 traces the evolution of the General Assembly, particularly through the Fourth Committee and the Committee on Information from Non-Self-Governing Territories, from a forum of cautious consultation into a primary arena of anti-colonial diplomacy. It examines the strategic deployment of Article 73(e) reporting obligations, the contestation over the criteria of "non-self-governing" status, and the coalitional politics through which Afro-Asian states, with tactical Soviet bloc support, expanded UN competence over colonial affairs.

Chapter 6 reconstructs the 1960 watershed: the diplomatic context of the "Year of Africa," the negotiation of Resolution 1514, its core provisions, and the parallel establishment of the Special Committee on Decolonization (the Committee of 24). It assesses the Declaration's normative significance in transforming self-determination into a quasi-legal right and its subsequent instrumentalization in disputes from Namibia to Western Sahara.

Chapter 7 quantitatively and qualitatively analyzes how the influx of newly independent states between 1955 and the mid-1970s reshaped voting balances, committee composition, and the substantive agenda of the General Assembly and specialized agencies. It examines how this transformation eroded Western dominance while remaining constrained by the persistence of Security Council vetoes and structural inequalities, and how the language of international politics gradually shifted from East–West ideological alignment to North–South questions of development, resource sovereignty, and economic justice.

Chapter 8 traces Third World diplomacy from the 1955 Bandung Conference through the founding of the Non-Aligned Movement at Belgrade in 1961 and the Group of 77 at UNCTAD I in 1964, to the NIEO declarations of 1974 and the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States. It analyzes the intellectual origins of the NIEO in dependency theory and structuralist economics, its key demands, and the reasons for its substantial rhetorical successes and its ultimate material defeat in the face of industrialized-state resistance, the 1980s debt crisis, and the rise of neoliberalism.

Chapter 9 examines the new operational functions that decolonization generated for international organizations: peacekeeping from Suez (1956) and the Congo (ONUC, 1960–1964) onward, the expansion of development assistance through UNDP and the specialized agencies, and the strengthening of human rights instruments responding to apartheid and racial discrimination. It shows how these new functions were themselves shaped, and sometimes constrained, by the politics of decolonization.

Chapter 10 synthesizes the report's findings, reassesses the entangled legacies of international organizations and decolonization, and reflects on the unfinished agendas of self-determination and the structural legacies of empire in contemporary global governance.

The cumulative argument advanced across these chapters can be stated, in anticipation, as follows: international organizations and decolonization mutually constituted one another across the twentieth century. The institutions of international governance were not simply repurposed by decolonization, nor did they simply absorb and contain it; they were transformed by it, even as they shaped the forms in which it could be pursued. The result was neither liberation nor unbroken domination but a complex, contested, and unfinished restructuring of world order — one whose legacies, both emancipatory and constraining, continue to define the international politics of the present.

# 2 The League of Nations Mandates System: Institutionalizing a 'Sacred Trust' and the Inadvertent Internationalization of Colonial Affairs

The introduction set out the central paradox that organizes this report: international organizations of the twentieth century operated simultaneously as instruments of imperial continuity and as platforms of anti-colonial contestation. Nowhere is this duality more starkly visible in its embryonic form than in the institutional experiment with which the modern era of international supervision over colonial governance began — the Mandates System established under Article 22 of the Covenant of the League of Nations. Designed at the Paris Peace Conference as a diplomatic compromise between Wilsonian rejection of outright annexation and the territorial appetites of the victorious imperial powers, the Mandates System was at once the first systematic attempt to subject colonial administration to multilateral oversight and a legal vehicle that legitimized continued European rule over former Ottoman and German possessions. To trace the operation of this system across its two-decade life — its legal architecture, its tripartite classification scheme, the practical work of its supervisory commission, the trajectories of the territories it governed, and the petitions it received — is to encounter, in their earliest institutional form, virtually every dynamic that would shape the longer twentieth-century history of international organizations and decolonization.

## 2.1 Origins and Legal Foundations of Article 22

The Mandates System emerged from a specific diplomatic predicament: the territorial settlement of the First World War required some disposition of the former colonial possessions of the defeated Ottoman and German Empires, but the manner of that disposition had become politically contentious. Britain and France, joined by the Dominions and Japan, expected to govern these territories as colonies and indeed had concluded wartime agreements (most notoriously the Sykes–Picot accord in the Middle East) anticipating outright partition. United States President Woodrow Wilson opposed such manoeuvres, regarding the wholesale annexation of former enemy territories as incompatible with the principles he had articulated in the Fourteen Points and with the wider Wilsonian critique of secret diplomacy and territorial aggrandizement.[^1] The Mandates System was the compromise that emerged from this collision: rather than being annexed by the victors, the former Ottoman and German possessions would be administered as **mandates** by individual states whose administration would in turn be subject to oversight by a permanent international commission.[^1]

The textual embodiment of this compromise, Article 22 of the Covenant, deserves close attention because its language structured the legal and political vocabulary of international supervision for the entire interwar period. Its opening paragraph announced that "to those colonies and territories which as a consequence of the late war have ceased to be under the sovereignty of the States which formerly governed them and which are inhabited by peoples not yet able to stand by themselves under the strenuous conditions of the modern world, there should be applied the principle that the well-being and development of such peoples form a sacred trust of civilization and that securities for the performance of this trust should be embodied in this Covenant."[^2] Several distinct legal innovations are folded into this single sentence.

The first innovation is the **non-annexation principle**. By declaring that the relevant territories had "ceased to be under the sovereignty" of the prior governing powers without simultaneously transferring sovereignty to the new administering powers, the Covenant constructed a category of territory that was neither colonial possession in the classical sense nor independent state. The mandate became, in effect, a *sui generis* legal status held in trust, with administering powers acting "on behalf of the League." As Paragraph 2 made explicit, "the tutelage of such peoples should be entrusted to advanced nations who by reason of their resources, their experience or their geographical position can best undertake this responsibility... and that this tutelage should be exercised by them as Mandatories on behalf of the League."[^2] The Permanent Mandates Commission would later play a decisive role in entrenching this principle, helping to establish that the mandates could not be annexed by the colonial powers and that they possessed a unique status under international law — a determination that would acquire profound significance in the post-1945 disputes over South West Africa.[^1]

The second innovation is the formula of the "**sacred trust of civilization**." Although the language is unmistakably saturated with the assumptions of the European civilizing mission — Paragraph 1 referred to "peoples not yet able to stand by themselves" — the formula simultaneously committed mandatory powers to a substantive normative standard against which their conduct could be measured, and which colonized petitioners would later invoke against the mandatories themselves. The phrase would acquire its own juridical afterlife: the International Court of Justice in 1950 would find "some support for its conclusion" regarding the continuing international status of South West Africa in the use of the words "sacred trust of civilization" in Article 22 of the Covenant.[^3]

The third innovation is the **principle of accountability through reporting and supervision**. Paragraph 7 required that "in every case of mandate, the Mandatory shall render to the Council an annual report in reference to the territory committed to its charge," and Paragraph 9 stipulated that "a permanent Commission shall be constituted to receive and examine the annual reports of the Mandatories and to advise the Council on all matters relating to the observance of the mandates."[^2] These provisions, however institutionally modest in their immediate effects, marked the first time that imperial powers were formally required to account for their colonial administration to an external multilateral body.[^1]

Situated within the broader history of international law, Article 22 represented a notable departure from the nineteenth-century model in which international law applied principally among "civilized" sovereign states while non-European peoples appeared either as objects of colonial acquisition or as candidates for inclusion subject to "standards of civilization." By inserting the well-being and development of non-European peoples into the constitutive document of the international organization itself, and by subjecting the administering powers to multilateral scrutiny, the Covenant began — without abandoning the civilizational hierarchies of its time — to construct a graduated, tutelary regime that lodged at least a formal international interest in the governance of non-self-governing peoples. The contradiction between this innovation and the imperial realities it accommodated would define the entire operation of the system that followed.

## 2.2 The A/B/C Classification: Hierarchies of 'Civilization' and Graduated Tutelage

Article 22 did not impose a uniform regime on all mandated territories. Instead, it articulated a tripartite classification — later codified in practice as Class A, Class B, and Class C mandates — that differentiated the obligations of the mandatory and the prospective trajectory of the territory according to assumptions about the developmental "stage" of its inhabitants and the territory's geographic and economic characteristics.[^2] Paragraph 3 stated the underlying principle in deceptively neutral language: "the character of the mandate must differ according to the stage of the development of the people, the geographical situation of the territory, its economic condition and other similar circumstances."[^2] In practice, this principle generated a starkly racialized hierarchy of readiness for self-government.

**Class A** mandates, addressed in Paragraph 4, comprised "certain communities formerly belonging to the Turkish Empire" that had "reached a stage of development where their existence as independent nations can be provisionally recognized, subject to the rendering of administrative advice and assistance by a Mandatory until such time as they are able to stand alone." Importantly, Paragraph 4 added that "the wishes of these communities must be a principal consideration in the selection of the Mandatory."[^2] In application, this category encompassed Palestine, Iraq, Syria, Lebanon, and Transjordan — territories whose "provisional recognition" as independent nations marked a meaningful legal distinction from outright colonial subjection, even as their actual administration under British and French mandate often diverged sharply from the autonomy that provisional recognition implied.

**Class B** mandates, treated in Paragraph 5, applied to "other peoples, especially those of Central Africa," for which the mandatory was made "responsible for the administration of the territory" under conditions designed to "guarantee freedom of conscience and religion," to prohibit "abuses such as the slave trade, the arms traffic and the liquor traffic," to prevent the establishment of military bases or the military training of natives beyond police and defensive purposes, and to "secure equal opportunities for the trade and commerce of other Members of the League."[^2] The territories that fell into this category — Tanganyika under Britain; Cameroon and Togo divided between Britain and France; Ruanda-Urundi under Belgium — were governed under direct administrative responsibility, with no equivalent of Class A's "provisional recognition," and the trajectory toward self-government was deferred indefinitely.

**Class C** mandates, addressed in Paragraph 6, comprised "territories, such as South West Africa and certain of the South Pacific Islands, which, owing to the sparseness of their population, or their small size, or their remoteness from the centres of civilization, or their geographical contiguity to the territory of the Mandatory, and other circumstances, can be best administered under the laws of the Mandatory as integral portions of its territory, subject to the safeguards above mentioned in the interests of the indigenous population."[^2] The language is telling: Class C mandates were to be governed "as integral portions" of the mandatory's territory — a formulation that came perilously close to authorizing the very annexation that Article 22 was meant to preclude. South West Africa under South Africa, New Guinea under Australia, Western Samoa under New Zealand, and the various Japanese-administered Pacific islands all fell within this category.

The following table synthesizes the principal features of the three classes:

| Class | Paragraph | Territories (representative) | Mandatory Powers | Legal Status | Administrative Regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | §4 | Palestine, Iraq, Transjordan, Syria, Lebanon | Britain, France | "Provisionally recognized" as independent nations; selection of mandatory to consider community wishes | Administrative "advice and assistance" pending capacity to "stand alone" |
| B | §5 | Tanganyika, Cameroon, Togo, Ruanda-Urundi | Britain, France, Belgium | Under direct administrative responsibility | Safeguards on slave/arms/liquor trades, military use, religious freedom, open door for trade |
| C | §6 | South West Africa, New Guinea, Western Samoa, Pacific islands | South Africa, Australia, New Zealand, Japan | Administered "as integral portions" of mandatory's territory | Most restrictive safeguards but most extensive integration into mandatory administration |

The classification was thus simultaneously a system of graduated tutelage and a codified hierarchy of "civilization." Several features deserve emphasis. First, the classification was explicitly racialized in its underlying assumptions, with the Middle Eastern territories — whose populations were understood to have heritages of statehood and high civilization legible to European observers — placed nearest the threshold of independence, and African and Pacific peoples relegated to indefinite tutelage. Second, the classification operated as a deferral mechanism: while it inscribed eventual self-government as the trajectory for Class A, it built no comparable telos into Class B or C, where the language of safeguards and tutelage could be sustained without temporal horizon. Third, the categories were politically motivated and not merely descriptive: Class C in particular reflected the demands of the Dominions and Japan to administer captured German territories as effective extensions of their own metropolitan jurisdiction, with the language of "integral portions" representing a concession to those demands within a system formally committed to non-annexation. The consequence was a system whose internal differentiations would generate sharply divergent trajectories under similar nominal legal framings — a divergence to which subsection 2.5 returns.

## 2.3 The Permanent Mandates Commission: Composition, Procedures, and Constrained Authority

The institutional embodiment of international supervision was the Permanent Mandates Commission (PMC), established on 1 December 1920 and headquartered at Geneva.[^1] Its existence was directly stipulated by Paragraph 9 of Article 22, which charged it with receiving and examining the annual reports of the mandatories and advising the Council "on all matters relating to the observance of the mandates."[^2]

The PMC's composition reflected the tensions of its founding compromise. The Commission had ten and later eleven members, with one additional seat held by the International Labour Organisation; four of the members represented mandatory powers, while seven were drawn from independent powers.[^1] Members served without fixed term, a feature that proved consequential for the Commission's developing institutional culture. Long-serving members included the Swiss lawyer William Rappard, who served for the Commission's entire active life of eighteen years; the Belgian Pierre Orts, also serving eighteen years; the Italian Marquis Alberto Theodoli, first chairman, for sixteen years; M. Palacios of Spain for fifteen years; Lord Lugard of Britain and the Dutch member Van Rees for thirteen years each; and Valentine Dannevig of Norway, who succeeded Anna Bugge-Wicksell in the Swedish–Norwegian seat, and Count José de Penha Garcia of Portugal, each for eleven years.[^1] Germany joined the PMC in October 1927 with the appointment of Ludwig Kastl, after having been "initially rebuffed" upon its accession to the League in 1926; Germany subsequently withdrew with its departure from the League in 1933, and Japan with its withdrawal in 1938.[^1]

The sociological character of this membership has been incisively analyzed by the historian Susan Pedersen, who has described the Commission as "very much an imperialists' club," noting that five out of nine initial members were retired colonial governors, ministers, or high officials, and that by the mid-1920s "the Commission began to resemble a spa for retired African governors."[^1] Pedersen has further documented that "Commission members believed in a civilizing mission."[^1] Yet she has simultaneously argued — and this is essential to grasping the system's paradoxical character — that despite this composition the organization in fact "act[ed] independently of states and established norms that constrained the behaviors of colonial powers."[^1] Three features of the Commission's working environment, on her account, generated this capacity for independent action: the lack of term limits, which "led to camaraderie among members and the development of expertise"; the authority derived from written texts, "which allowed the members to perform impartiality"; and the publication of the organization's deliberations.[^1] In her judgment the Commission was at its most influential in 1932.[^1]

Procedurally, the Commission met in sessions once or twice a year to consider the annual reports of the mandatories. Within any session, any number of meetings could be held, usually in private although any of these could be public; extraordinary sessions could be convened in special circumstances. The Commission held its first session from 4 to 8 October 1921, and its thirty-seventh and final session from 12 to 21 December 1939.[^1] At its first session, the members established several foundational principles that significantly expanded the de facto scope of their work: they determined that they had the right to examine all aspects of administration in the mandated territories — not merely those specifically enumerated in the Covenant — that a comprehensive list of questions should be put to all mandate powers, and that mandate powers were obliged to forward all legislation affecting the territories to the Commission.[^1] These self-determined working principles, modest as they sound, converted the formal duty of reporting into a regime of sustained interrogation in which mandatory representatives were called to Geneva to answer questions on the record.

The constraints on the PMC's authority were nevertheless severe. It had "no power to enforce its recommendations" and could not compel compliance.[^1] Its function was advisory: it advised the Council, which alone possessed political authority. Furthermore, the legislative influence exerted by Britain and France within the PMC, combined with the absence of executive authority, meant that "these powers were able to govern their mandated territories in much the same way as their other colonies."[^1] Oversight, in summary, "functioned mainly through reporting, debate, and public accountability rather than direct intervention."[^1]

The synthesis that emerges from these features is paradoxical but coherent. The Commission was institutionally weak in the conventional sense: it lacked enforcement powers, it was numerically dominated by individuals drawn from imperial establishments, and it operated under significant procedural constraints. Yet its members developed expertise through long tenure, its proceedings produced authoritative written records that circulated internationally, and its existence established that mandatories were subject to questioning, however much they might evade its substance. The Commission represented, in the formulation that captures its function best, "one of the first multilateral international mechanisms through which Britain and France were formally required to account for their colonial administration to an external body."[^1] Whether that accounting bound the conduct of mandatory powers in any substantive sense, or merely registered grievances in international space, is a question whose answer varies sharply across cases — and to which subsequent subsections turn.

## 2.4 Mandatory Practice: Imperial Administration under International Supervision

The practical operation of the mandates in the territories themselves diverged in important ways from the elevated language of "sacred trust." With the partial exception of the Class A territories, mandate administration in the interwar decades closely resembled conventional colonial rule in its land policies, labor regimes, fiscal extraction, and reliance on indirect administration through co-opted indigenous authorities. The fundamental structural reason for this congruence has been clearly identified: because of "the significant legislative influence exerted by Britain and France within the PMC, coupled with the commission's lack of executive authority, these powers were able to govern their mandated territories in much the same way as their other colonies."[^1]

In the British-administered Class A mandates of the Middle East — Palestine, Iraq, and Transjordan — administration was shaped by the imperatives of strategic communications, oil interests, and, in Palestine, by the additional obligation incurred under the Balfour Declaration to facilitate the establishment of a Jewish national home, an obligation incorporated into the mandate text itself. In Iraq, Britain pursued a strategy of indirect rule through the Hashemite monarchy installed in 1921, which combined formal sovereignty under treaty arrangements with effective British control over defense, finance, and foreign affairs — an arrangement that would culminate, as discussed below, in Iraq's nominal independence and League membership in 1932. In Tanganyika, the principal British Class B mandate, the colonial administration of Sir Donald Cameron developed indirect rule along Lugardian lines, drawing explicitly on the practical philosophy that Lord Lugard himself articulated and that he carried, in the same period, into his role on the Permanent Mandates Commission.

French administration in Syria and Lebanon, by contrast, embraced direct administrative intervention and the political fragmentation of mandate territories into smaller administrative units (the États of Damascus, Aleppo, the Alawites, and so forth), a strategy that provoked intense resistance and culminated in the Great Syrian Revolt of 1925–1927, whose brutal suppression — including the artillery bombardment of Damascus — generated significant criticism in Geneva. In the African Class B mandates of Cameroon and Togo, France integrated mandate administration with the broader framework of *Afrique-Équatoriale française* and *Afrique-Occidentale française*. Belgian administration of Ruanda-Urundi attached the mandate administratively to the neighboring Belgian Congo and continued to operate through the existing hierarchies of Tutsi monarchy and chieftaincy.

The Class C mandates exhibited the strongest tendencies toward effective annexation. South Africa governed South West Africa essentially as a fifth province, extending settler land alienation, the system of native reserves, and pass and labor regulations developed in the Union itself. Australia administered the former German New Guinea and Nauru with comparable directness. Japan integrated the Micronesian islands — the Marshalls, Carolines, and Marianas — into its broader imperial framework under the administration of the South Seas Bureau (Nan'yō-chō). New Zealand administered Western Samoa with an intrusive directness that, as the petitions discussed below would reveal, generated significant indigenous resistance.

Yet the international framework imposed constraints that, however limited, were not entirely negligible. The Permanent Mandates Commission's foundational determination at its first session that it could examine all aspects of administration in mandated territories — combined with the obligation of mandatories to forward all relevant legislation — meant that administrative practices were on record in Geneva and subject to questioning.[^1] The Commission played "a key role in establishing that the mandates could not be annexed by the colonial powers," and helped to establish that they "had a unique status under international law."[^1] The Covenant's specific prohibitions in Class B mandates — against the slave trade, the arms traffic, and the liquor traffic, and against military training of natives "for other than police purposes and the defence of territory" — and the requirement of an "open door" for the trade of other League members imposed at least nominal constraints on mandatory autonomy that did not apply in conventional colonies.[^2] How tightly these constraints bit in practice is contested; the Commission's lack of enforcement powers meant that even egregious violations could be registered but not directly redressed.[^1] But the constraints existed, and their breach could be publicized.

## 2.5 Divergent Trajectories: Class A Middle Eastern Mandates versus Class B/C African and Pacific Mandates

The differential trajectories of the three mandate classes during the interwar period are themselves striking evidence of how the formal classification structured outcomes. Class A territories moved, however contestedly, toward formal sovereignty within the period; Class B and C territories did not. This divergence was not preordained by the populations or capacities of the territories themselves; it was substantially produced by the legal architecture of the system and by the political strategies that the classification enabled and constrained.

Among the Class A mandates, Iraq presented the paradigmatic case of accelerated movement toward formal independence. The British mandate was converted into a treaty-based relationship in 1930 and Iraq was admitted to the League of Nations in 1932 — the only mandate territory to achieve formal sovereignty and League membership during the interwar period. The admission was, however, less a vindication of the mandates' developmental promise than a negotiated arrangement that preserved substantial British strategic interests through the post-mandate treaty regime, including military basing rights and continuing influence over Iraqi foreign and military policy. Syria and Lebanon, under French mandate, were promised independence under treaties negotiated in 1936 but not ratified by France before the outbreak of war; their formal independence would be secured only during and after the Second World War. Transjordan, separated administratively from Palestine in 1921, moved toward sovereignty under the Hashemite Emir Abdullah but achieved it only in 1946. Palestine, uniquely, was scheduled neither for independence under a defined timetable nor for absorption into mandatory administration; the Balfour-derived obligation toward the Jewish national home, combined with mounting Arab–Jewish conflict, produced the trajectory toward the UN partition plan of 1947 and the dissolution of the mandate in 1948.

The Class B and Class C mandates, by contrast, exhibited no comparable movement toward independence within the League era. The Class B African territories remained under unchanged mandatory administration throughout the interwar period and were transferred to the UN Trusteeship System after 1945, where their movement toward independence proceeded in step with the broader African decolonization of the 1950s and 1960s. The Class C territories included the case that would generate the most intractable post-1945 legal contest: South Africa's South West Africa, which is discussed in detail below.

The factors that produced these divergent trajectories were political, demographic, strategic, and ideological. Politically, the Class A formula of "provisional recognition" had inscribed independence as the explicit telos of the mandate, providing nationalist movements with a normative resource they could deploy against the mandatory in Geneva and in the metropole. Demographically and historically, the Middle Eastern territories were governed under the assumption — itself contestable but operative in European elite discourse — that they possessed political and administrative traditions compatible with the European model of statehood. Strategically, British policy in the Middle East privileged the negotiation of treaty-based arrangements that traded formal sovereignty for substantive military and economic privileges. Ideologically, the racialized hierarchies that had produced the A/B/C classification continued to operate in determining which territories were considered "ready" for self-government and which were not — and on this metric African and Pacific peoples were systematically positioned at the back of the developmental queue.

The legacy of these divergent trajectories for the broader history of decolonization is significant. The mandates regime helped institutionalize the temporal unevenness of decolonization: even within the same international supervisory framework, different territories were placed on entirely different trajectories, with Middle Eastern Class A independence achieved or scheduled within two decades, while African Class B and Class C territories awaited a further generation. The pattern foreshadowed the uneven temporalities of post-1945 decolonization, which would unfold across multiple distinct waves rather than as a single global moment.

## 2.6 The Petition System and the Voices of the Colonized

If the annual reporting regime was the channel through which mandatory administrations spoke to the Commission, the petition procedure was the channel — narrower, more contested, and more procedurally constrained — through which other voices could be heard. The Permanent Mandates Commission's reception and examination of petitions transformed Geneva, in unforeseen ways, into an international stage upon which colonial grievances could be publicized.

The procedure operated under significant restrictions: petitions from inhabitants of mandated territories were generally required to be transmitted through the mandatory power, which had the opportunity to comment, and the Commission's response was advisory rather than executive. Despite these restrictions, the petition system generated a substantial corpus of complaints that brought specific abuses, policies, and aspirations onto the international record.

Petitioners were diverse. They included indigenous nationalist movements and their advocates in the Class A Middle Eastern territories — Syrian nationalists protesting French administrative fragmentation and the suppression of the 1925–1927 revolt; Palestinian Arab leaders contesting the implementation of the Balfour Declaration; and Iraqi voices both supporting and contesting the terms of the British relationship. They included indigenous communities and minority groups in Class C territories — the Bondelswarts and Rehoboth peoples of South West Africa, whose petitions challenged South African administration; Samoan petitioners associated with the Mau movement, whose campaign of civil disobedience contested New Zealand's administrative practice. They included religious and missionary networks operating across mandate territories, which used the petition procedure to amplify humanitarian concerns. And they included anti-slavery societies and humanitarian advocates whose engagement with the Commission articulated a broader claim to international civil society standing in colonial matters.

The strategic significance of the petition procedure lay less in its capacity to compel specific remedies — which was minimal — than in its capacity to internationalize grievances that would otherwise have remained confined to the bilateral relationship between metropole and colony. The Commission was required to consider petitions, mandatories were required to respond to them, and the published records of the Commission carried the substance of these exchanges into European parliamentary debate, missionary publications, and the international press. The very act of petitioning Geneva inscribed the colonized petitioner as an addressable subject of international politics, capable of speaking past the mandatory to a wider international audience. Petitioners, moreover, frequently invoked the very legal categories that the Mandates System had inscribed in the Covenant — the "sacred trust," the principle of non-annexation, the safeguards against abuse — turning the mandatory's own normative commitments into the rhetorical instruments of critique.

The asymmetries built into the procedure should not be underestimated. The requirement that petitions be transmitted through the mandatory created an inherent vulnerability to suppression and editing. The Commission's reluctance to receive oral testimony from petitioners in Geneva, except in narrow circumstances, restricted the direct voice that colonized petitioners could exercise. The dominance of mandatory representatives in the proceedings and the Commission's procedural deference to those representatives meant that petitions were typically considered against the mandatory's response and within a framework of presumed mandatory good faith. Nevertheless, the petition procedure represented the most significant institutional opening through which non-state and colonized voices accessed the international system during the interwar period, and it established a procedural template that the UN Trusteeship Council would inherit and substantially liberalize.

## 2.7 Transnational Advocacy and the Internationalization of Colonial Affairs

Around the formal procedures of the Permanent Mandates Commission, an ecosystem of non-state actors developed whose engagement with the mandates regime extended its political reach well beyond the formal sessions in Geneva. Anti-slavery societies, missionary federations, humanitarian organizations, minority rights advocates, and early pan-African and pan-Asian networks operated in a complex relationship with the Commission — using its publicity to amplify their own campaigns, supplying it with information that mandatory powers would not voluntarily disclose, and translating its findings into political pressure in metropolitan capitals through parliamentary networks, press coverage, and public campaigns.

Several features of this transnational ecosystem deserve emphasis. First, its existence depended on the Commission's practice of publishing its deliberations, which Pedersen has identified as one of the structural sources of the PMC's capacity for independent action.[^1] The published record converted Geneva into a clearing-house through which information about mandate administration circulated to a wide range of metropolitan and international audiences, and around which campaigns could be organized. Second, the participation of these non-state actors prefigured later, more developed forms of transnational civil society engagement with international institutions — what would, in the post-1945 era, be elaborated through NGO consultative arrangements at the UN. Third, the engagement was structurally constrained by the Eurocentric assumptions about legitimate speech that pervaded the international order: humanitarian and missionary organizations based in metropolitan capitals could approach the Commission with relative ease, while colonized voices typically required mediation through such advocates or through the formal petition procedure.

The result was an early form of internationalization of colonial affairs — a process in which colonial governance, previously treated as a matter of metropolitan domestic policy or bilateral concern, became a subject of multilateral debate, publication, and campaign. This internationalization did not, in the interwar period, displace the imperial order; mandatory powers continued to govern their territories and most petitions failed to elicit remedies. But it did establish the principle that colonial governance could legitimately be the subject of international scrutiny, and it constructed the institutional pathways through which that scrutiny could be conducted. Both the principle and the pathways would prove consequential for the post-1945 transformation analyzed in subsequent chapters.

## 2.8 Limits, Failures, and Crises of the System

The structural weaknesses of the Mandates System were exposed repeatedly across its two decades of operation, and the catalogue of failures is essential to a balanced assessment. The Commission's lack of enforcement powers meant that egregious mandatory abuses could be registered but not redressed. The 1922 Bondelswarts massacre in South West Africa, in which South African forces used aerial bombardment and machine guns to suppress a tax revolt among the Bondelswarts people with significant loss of life, became a notorious case in Geneva: the Commission examined the matter, raised questions, and registered concern, but it lacked any mechanism to compel substantive change in South African administration. The Great Syrian Revolt of 1925–1927, suppressed by French forces with the bombardment of Damascus and the use of overwhelming military force, similarly generated examination in Geneva but no effective international constraint on French conduct. In the Class C Pacific, the petitions and campaign of the Samoan Mau movement against New Zealand administration produced extensive engagement but no fundamental reform.

The political constitution of the Commission compounded its formal weakness. The legislative dominance of Britain and France within the PMC, combined with the broader configuration of League politics, meant that the principal mandatory powers were also among the principal architects of the supervisory regime to which they were nominally subject — an arrangement that "allowed [them] to govern their mandated territories in much the same way as their other colonies."[^1] The Commission was dependent on mandatory cooperation for the information on which its work depended, on metropolitan goodwill for the conditions of its operation, and on the political authority of the League Council for any substantive response to its findings.

The international crises of the 1930s eroded what authority the system possessed. Germany's withdrawal from the League in 1933 ended its participation on the PMC and reopened the political question of restitution of former German colonies that the mandates regime had nominally settled.[^1] Japan's withdrawal in 1938 similarly ended its membership on the Commission and removed one of the original mandatory powers from international supervision.[^1] The collapse of collective security in the late 1930s further hollowed the political framework within which the Commission operated. The PMC's last meeting was held at its thirty-seventh session from 12 to 21 December 1939, only months after the outbreak of the war that would end the League's effective existence.[^1]

The case that came to symbolize most starkly the limits of the mandates regime — and that would extend its legal afterlife into the UN era — was South West Africa. The territory had been administered by South Africa under a Class C mandate from 1920, with the Covenant's permission to govern it "as integral portions" of South African territory creating an architectural ambiguity that South Africa progressively exploited. South African administration extended settler colonization, segregationist legislation, and the system of native reserves into the territory; the Bondelswarts and Rehoboth communities were among those whose petitions challenged this trajectory. By the late 1940s, with the League dissolved and the UN's trusteeship arrangements being negotiated, South Africa declined to convert the mandate into a trusteeship and sought, instead, to incorporate the territory formally into the Union. This refusal precipitated the long sequence of UN General Assembly resolutions, International Court of Justice advisory opinions, and contentious proceedings that culminated in the 1971 ICJ Advisory Opinion on Namibia and the eventual independence of Namibia in 1990. The 1950 ICJ opinion that anchored this jurisprudence drew explicitly, as noted above, on "the use of the words 'sacred trust of civilization' in Article 22 of the Covenant" to ground its conclusion that South West Africa retained an international status that South Africa could not unilaterally dissolve.[^3] The Mandates System's limits, in this case, produced not merely failure but a foundational legal legacy: the very weakness of interwar enforcement generated the post-1945 legal and political contests through which the principle of international supervisory responsibility was substantially strengthened.

## 2.9 Legacies: From Geneva to the UN Trusteeship Template

To assess the Mandates System's bequest to subsequent international practice requires holding two judgments in simultaneous view. On the one hand, the system was substantially complicit with empire: it accommodated, regularized, and to some extent legitimized the continued administration of former Ottoman and German territories by victorious imperial powers; it codified racialized hierarchies of "civilization" through the A/B/C classification; and it lacked the enforcement powers necessary to translate its normative principles into substantive constraint on mandatory conduct. On the other hand, the system inaugurated institutional innovations whose subsequent elaboration would prove central to the post-1945 architecture of decolonization.

The principal innovations were five. First, the **principle of international supervision over colonial governance** — that certain territories were not the unfettered domestic concern of their administering powers but were instead subject to multilateral scrutiny on behalf of the international community. Second, the **legal status of *sui generis* internationalized territory**, embodied in the principle of non-annexation, which the PMC played a key role in establishing.[^1] Third, the **machinery of periodic reporting and questioning**, which converted formal obligation into sustained interrogation. Fourth, the **petition procedure**, which constructed a channel — narrow but consequential — through which non-state and colonized voices could enter the international system. Fifth, the **public character of supervision**, with proceedings and findings published and circulated internationally, generating the conditions under which transnational advocacy could operate.

Each of these innovations would be inherited, modified, and in important respects strengthened by the institutional architecture that emerged from the San Francisco Conference of 1945. The Trusteeship Council under Chapters XII and XIII of the UN Charter would inherit the supervisory function but operate with a more explicit telos of self-government or independence and with procedural improvements including direct oral testimony from petitioners and visiting missions to trust territories. The Chapter XI Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories would extend the principle of international interest beyond the narrow category of formal trust territories to the broader universe of non-self-governing peoples — a normative innovation whose subsequent contestation in the General Assembly and the Committee on Information from Non-Self-Governing Territories would form the substance of the analysis in Chapter 5.

The deeper legacy, however, is conceptual and political. The Mandates System established the proposition that colonial governance could be a legitimate subject of multilateral debate, normative judgment, and procedural review — that imperial powers were no longer entitled to claim that the administration of dependent peoples was a sovereign matter beyond international concern. This proposition was developed within an institutional framework that remained substantially imperial in its composition and practice, and it was enacted through procedures whose constraints often nullified their substantive promise. But the proposition itself, once articulated and institutionalized, proved difficult to retract. It was on this proposition — and through the modified institutional vocabulary that the League bequeathed — that the more far-reaching anti-colonial politics of the post-1945 era would be conducted.

Two final observations frame the transition to subsequent chapters. First, the Mandates System operated alongside, and in implicit dialogue with, a different mode of anti-colonial internationalism that constructed itself from outside official institutions — most paradigmatically the League Against Imperialism founded at Brussels in 1927, whose history and significance constitute the subject of Chapter 3. The interwar internationalization of colonial affairs proceeded along two distinct tracks — the supervisory and the counter-mobilizational — whose interactions and contrasts illuminate the broader politics of the period. Second, the Mandates System's particular gift to the post-1945 order was less the substance of its achievements, which were modest, than the procedural and conceptual template it supplied: international commissions, periodic reports, petitions, the legal category of internationalized territory, and the principle that the welfare of dependent peoples was a matter of international concern. The reconstruction of these inheritances within the UN Charter system, analyzed in Chapter 4, would carry the institutional logic of Geneva into a transformed political context in which the universalist commitments of the founding generation would, over the following decades, be appropriated and radicalized by an emerging anti-colonial majority.

[^3]: The United Nations, South West Africa and the Law.
[^1]: Permanent Mandates Commission, Wikipedia.
[^2]: Article 22 of the Covenant of the League of Nations.

# 3 Anti-Imperialist Counter-Currents: The League Against Imperialism and Transnational Anti-Colonial Mobilization

Chapter 2 concluded by observing that the interwar internationalization of colonial affairs proceeded along two distinct tracks: a supervisory track institutionalized within the League of Nations Mandates System, and a counter-mobilizational track constructed by anti-colonial activists from outside, and often against, the official multilateral order. This chapter takes up the second of those tracks. Its subject is the League Against Imperialism and for National Independence (LAI), founded at the Palais d'Egmont in Brussels in February 1927 and dissolved a decade later under the combined pressure of Cominternal recalibration and state repression. The LAI was at once a singular organizational experiment — the first self-consciously global anti-imperialist front capable of convening Asian, African, Latin American, and European delegates around a common platform — and a paradigmatic counter-institution to the Geneva-based regime examined in the previous chapter. Its rhetoric explicitly rejected the civilizational hierarchies that organized Article 22; its founding congress assembled, in a single hall, voices that the Permanent Mandates Commission's procedurally constrained petition system rendered audible only intermittently and through metropolitan mediation; and its operational politics sought to construct, outside the procedures of the League Council, an alternative international publicity for the grievances of colonized peoples.

The chapter approaches the LAI not as a footnote to interwar communism but as a constitutive moment in the long durée of anti-colonial internationalism — one whose organizational templates, personal networks, and discursive innovations connected the nineteenth-century pan-movements with the Bandung Conference of 1955 and the Third World project that occupies Chapters 7 and 8. At the same time, the chapter resists both hagiography and dismissal. The LAI was a fragile and ultimately short-lived organization. Its hybrid ideological architecture — fusing Comintern strategy with non-communist nationalist movements — generated extraordinary initial breadth but contained unresolved tensions that fractured the organization within two years of its founding. Its operational reach was constrained by financial dependence on Soviet-aligned resources, by metropolitan surveillance and repression, and by the geographic dispersal of its national sections. Holding these limitations in view alongside its achievements is essential to the balanced assessment that this report's interpretive framework demands.

A methodological caveat is appropriate at the outset. The historiography of the LAI is uneven, and the present chapter is constrained by the absence of recent specialised scholarly material in the reference base supplied for this report. Several factual claims that appear in standard accounts of the LAI — for instance, the figure of 174 delegates from 37 countries, the precise composition of national delegations, and the chronology of the second congress at Frankfurt in 1929 — are widely cited in the secondary literature but cannot be independently verified against the search results available for this section. Where such claims are reproduced below, they are presented as standard accounts whose evidentiary support lies outside the immediate reference base, and the chapter signals these limitations where they bear on the substance of the argument. The analytical claims advanced in the chapter — concerning the LAI's structural relationship to the official internationalism examined in Chapter 2, its position within the longer trajectory of anti-colonial mobilization, and its bequest to post-1945 Third World politics — are developed in dialogue with the framework established in the introduction and Chapter 2, and do not depend on the particular numerical claims whose verification falls outside the available evidence base.

## 3.1 The Brussels Congress of 1927: Founding Context and Convening Coalition

The convocation of the Brussels Congress in February 1927 cannot be understood apart from the immediately preceding wave of anti-colonial agitation that had transformed the political horizons of the mid-1920s. The Rif War in Morocco, waged by Abd el-Krim against Spanish and then combined Spanish–French forces from 1921 through 1926, demonstrated the capacity of an organized indigenous resistance to inflict significant military reverses on European colonial armies and drew sustained metropolitan-left solidarity. The Great Syrian Revolt of 1925–1927, suppressed by French forces with the bombardment of Damascus, generated — as Chapter 2 noted — intense criticism in Geneva but exposed the supervisory machinery of the Permanent Mandates Commission as incapable of constraining mandatory military conduct. The Chinese revolution of 1925–1927, with its mass nationalist mobilization, the Kuomintang–Communist united front, and the Northern Expedition launched in mid-1926, brought into European consciousness the prospect of a large continental polity reorganizing itself under explicitly anti-imperial auspices. The British general strike of May 1926 demonstrated the political volatility of the imperial metropole itself. And in India, the constitutional politics of the Indian National Congress was intersecting with mass mobilization in forms that would crystallize, in the years immediately following Brussels, into the launch of the Civil Disobedience movement.

The accumulation of these crises created what might be termed a demand for transnational coordination: nationalist movements operating in separate regional circuits, with limited mutual contact and few channels for amplifying their grievances within metropolitan political debates, increasingly recognized the value of a common platform. The supply side of that equation was provided by a particular configuration of European left-wing organizational capacity centered on the German communist publicist Willi Münzenberg and the Comintern-affiliated International Workers' Aid (Internationale Arbeiterhilfe, IAH), an organization that Münzenberg had developed through the 1920s into an extensive front network with print, propaganda, and convening capacities. Münzenberg's organizational role in convening the Brussels Congress represented an extension of this front-building practice into the explicitly anti-imperial domain — drawing on Comintern strategic interest in cultivating relationships with nationalist movements outside the formal communist party structure while presenting itself, to non-communist participants, as a broad and ostensibly non-sectarian convening platform.

The Brussels Congress, held at the Palais d'Egmont over six days in February 1927, is widely described in the secondary literature as having gathered some 174 delegates from approximately 37 countries, though as noted above the precise figures lie outside the verifiable reference base for this chapter. The composition of the assembly, however, can be characterized in broader analytical terms with confidence. The delegates included representatives of Asian nationalist movements (the Indian National Congress, the Indonesian Perhimpoenan Indonesia, Chinese Kuomintang and communist representatives), of Middle Eastern and North African nationalist currents (Egyptian, Syrian, and Maghrebi voices), of Latin American anti-imperialist movements responding to the post-1898 expansion of United States hemispheric intervention, and of African and African-diasporic political networks. They were joined by European left-wing intellectuals, parliamentarians, and trade union officials — figures such as the British Labour MP George Lansbury, the French novelist Henri Barbusse, and Albert Einstein, who served on the organization's honorary presidium — whose presence lent the proceedings metropolitan respectability and constructed a bridge between the assembled anti-colonial movements and the European political milieu in which their grievances would have to be publicized.

The congress thus operated simultaneously on several registers. It was a public spectacle, designed to demonstrate the existence of a transnational anti-imperialist constituency and to generate metropolitan publicity. It was a working political conference, adopting resolutions, electing leadership, and laying the groundwork for an ongoing organizational presence (headquartered in Berlin until 1933). And it was a site of mutual recognition and political education, in which nationalist leaders who had previously operated within largely separate regional circuits encountered one another, identified shared adversaries, and began to articulate the common categories — "imperialism," "national independence," "self-determination of peoples" — through which their distinct struggles could be theorized as part of a single global phenomenon. The significance of the latter dimension for the subsequent trajectory of anti-colonial politics is difficult to overstate, and is examined more fully in subsection 3.3.

## 3.2 Ideological Foundations: Comintern Strategy, Nationalist Mobilization, and the United Front

The ideological architecture of the LAI is best understood as a strategic alignment of two distinct, ultimately incompatible political logics: the Comintern's "united front" approach to colonial liberation in the mid-1920s, and the tactical openness of non-communist anti-colonial nationalists to engagement with Soviet-aligned networks. The temporary congruence of these logics generated the LAI's founding coalition; their subsequent divergence, examined in subsection 3.5, ensured the organization's institutional fragility.

The Comintern's interest in colonial questions had a doctrinal foundation in Leninist theories of imperialism, which identified the colonial periphery as the structural weak link of advanced capitalism and accorded to anti-colonial movements a strategic significance comparable to that of the metropolitan proletariat. The practical translation of this analytical framework into political strategy passed through several phases in the early Comintern, most significantly the Second Congress of 1920, where Lenin's "Theses on the National and Colonial Questions" debated with M. N. Roy's supplementary theses had outlined a strategy of cooperation between communist parties and "bourgeois-democratic" nationalist movements in colonial territories — what subsequently became known as the united front "from above."
