This strategy guided Comintern engagement with the Kuomintang in China through the mid-1920s, justified the cultivation of relationships with nationalist intellectuals in India and elsewhere, and provided the doctrinal frame within which Münzenberg's convocation of the Brussels Congress could be understood as a legitimate exercise of revolutionary strategy.

From the perspective of non-communist nationalists, engagement with Comintern-affiliated networks made tactical sense for several reasons. The Soviet Union, as the only major state offering rhetorical and at moments material support to anti-colonial movements during the interwar period, represented an indispensable interlocutor for nationalist leaders seeking international leverage against their metropolitan adversaries. The organizational capacity that Münzenberg and the IAH could deploy — convening capabilities, publication networks, translation services, financial logistics — was not available in comparable form from any other interwar political current. And the discursive framework that the Comintern offered — "imperialism" as a unified global system, "national liberation" as a category integrating distinct colonial struggles, "self-determination of peoples" as a rhetorical principle that the Soviet Union endorsed without the Wilsonian civilizational qualifications — provided non-communist nationalists with conceptual resources that they could not draw from the Geneva-centered vocabulary of mandates and sacred trust.

The hybrid ideological character of the LAI was thus its defining feature. It assembled communists committed to a global revolutionary project alongside nationalists committed primarily to the political independence of their own territories; it brought together pan-Africanists, pan-Asianists, and Latin American anti-imperialists whose analytical frameworks differed substantially from Leninist orthodoxy; and it sought to construct, through the work of the congress and the operational follow-up, a common platform whose terms were sufficiently capacious to accommodate this heterogeneity without immediately exposing its internal contradictions. The principal unresolved tensions concerned the relative priority of class struggle and national struggle (with communists privileging the former and nationalists the latter, while united-front strategy attempted to subordinate the difference to immediate operational cooperation); the political evaluation of indigenous bourgeoisies in colonial territories (whom Comintern strategy treated as tactical allies but whom communist orthodoxy would later identify as adversaries of the working class); and the centrality of the Soviet Union as the political reference point of anti-imperialism (which non-communist nationalists could accept tactically but not unconditionally).

These tensions were not the contingent product of personal incompatibilities; they were structural features of a coalition whose component parts pursued different political projects under a common rhetorical canopy. The success of the Brussels Congress and the operational viability of the LAI in its first phase depended on holding these tensions in suspension. Their eruption — precipitated by the Comintern's strategic shift after 1928, analyzed in subsection 3.5 — would dissolve the coalition's institutional form, even as the discursive innovations it had produced continued to circulate within both communist and non-communist anti-colonial currents.

## 3.3 Geographies of Participation: Asian, African, Latin American, and Diasporic Delegations

The geographical breadth of LAI participation is essential to understanding what the Brussels Congress accomplished and what it did not. The congress was, on any account, the most geographically diverse anti-colonial gathering convened to that date, bringing into a single political space movements whose previous transnational contacts had been mediated through metropolitan intermediaries or had operated within more narrowly regional pan-movements.

The Asian delegations were among the most politically consequential. The Indian National Congress was represented by Jawaharlal Nehru, then in his late thirties and acting in a delegated capacity on behalf of the Congress while resident in Europe. Nehru's participation at Brussels has been widely identified in the historiography as a formative experience in his political education, exposing him to the international dimensions of the anti-colonial struggle and to the strategic vocabulary of "imperialism" as a global system — vocabulary that would resurface in his subsequent writings, including the autobiographical and historical works of the 1930s and 1940s, and in the diplomatic positioning of independent India after 1947. Indonesian nationalism was represented through the Perhimpoenan Indonesia, the Indonesian student association in the Netherlands, and figures including Mohammad Hatta, who would later serve as the first Vice-President of independent Indonesia and as a co-architect, with Sukarno, of the Bandung Conference of 1955. Chinese participation reflected the late phase of the first Kuomintang–Communist united front, with delegations representing both currents in a moment of strained but still operative collaboration that would break down catastrophically in April 1927 with Chiang Kai-shek's Shanghai massacre, only weeks after the Brussels Congress.

Middle Eastern and North African participation was structured by the immediate political conjunctures examined in the preceding subchapter. Egyptian nationalist delegates spoke from the experience of the partial sovereignty negotiated in the 1922 Anglo-Egyptian arrangement and the ongoing struggle over the residual British presence; Syrian and Maghrebi participants brought the experience of recent or ongoing revolts under French rule. African and African-diasporic participation, while quantitatively more modest than Asian, was politically significant: it included figures connected to early pan-African networks and to the Negro Workers' International (the International Trade Union Committee of Negro Workers, established in the early 1930s as part of the broader Comintern-aligned trade union architecture, would draw partly on contacts initiated through the LAI), and provided a bridge between the European anti-imperialist milieu and African and African-American political constituencies whose engagement with international politics would intensify during the 1930s, particularly in response to the Italian invasion of Ethiopia in 1935.

Latin American participation reflected the particular character of the Western Hemisphere as a region whose principal anti-imperialist target was the United States rather than European colonial powers. Delegates from movements responding to U.S. interventions in Nicaragua, Haiti, Cuba, and elsewhere brought to Brussels a regional anti-imperialist tradition with its own intellectual genealogies — including the late-nineteenth-century Cuban revolutionary tradition associated with José Martí and the pan-Hispanic anti-imperialist currents of the early twentieth century — that intersected with but did not derive from European Leninist theories of imperialism. The participation of these movements gave the LAI a hemispheric breadth that the more Europe-centered anti-colonial currents of the interwar period typically lacked, and it foreshadowed the multi-regional architecture of the post-1945 Non-Aligned Movement.

The cumulative effect of these participations was to construct, in the work of the congress itself, what previous transnational anti-colonial gatherings had only intermittently approached: a working recognition that struggles previously theorized within regional or pan-civilizational frames (pan-African, pan-Asian, Latin American Bolivarian) were articulations of a single global phenomenon — imperialism — whose contestation required correspondingly global coordination. This recognition was not, in 1927, fully theorized or institutionally embodied; the LAI would not achieve the sustained operational capacity that the recognition implied. But it was articulated, and the personal networks formed at Brussels carried it forward. The post-1945 Afro-Asian and Third World political vocabularies, whose elaboration this report examines in Chapters 7 and 8, drew significantly on the conceptual and personal infrastructure that the Brussels Congress had begun to construct.

## 3.4 Principal Campaigns and Operational Activities

Translating the Brussels declarations into sustained transnational political action proved considerably more difficult than convening the founding congress, and the LAI's operational history between 1927 and the mid-1930s is best characterized as a series of campaigns rather than a continuous program of unified activity. The organization's principal coordinating offices were located in Berlin, where Münzenberg's organizational infrastructure was concentrated and where the LAI's general secretariat was based for most of the period before the Nazi seizure of power in 1933.

Several campaigns can be identified as the LAI's principal substantive engagements. The first concerned India, where the LAI maintained ongoing solidarity with the Indian National Congress and, after the launch of the Civil Disobedience movement in 1930, with the mass mobilization led by Gandhi against the salt tax and the broader British administrative apparatus. A particular focus of LAI activism was the Meerut Conspiracy Case, in which the British colonial government arrested and prosecuted, from 1929, a substantial group of Indian trade union and communist activists on conspiracy charges; the prolonged trial, which extended through the early 1930s, generated extensive international solidarity campaigns in which the LAI participated through publicity, fundraising, and the coordination of metropolitan-left support. The Meerut campaign demonstrated the LAI's capacity to amplify a colonial repression episode into a substantial item of international political controversy, though the underlying juridical outcome — the conviction of most defendants — was not affected by the international mobilization.

A second focus was China, where the political fragmentation following the 1927 collapse of the Kuomintang–Communist united front and the subsequent Chinese Civil War created complex challenges for an international solidarity organization whose initial Chinese contacts spanned currents that had now become mutual adversaries. The LAI's China-related work in this period concentrated increasingly on the communist side of the conflict, reflecting both the Comintern's strategic priorities and the progressive narrowing of the LAI's coalition examined in subsection 3.5.

A third campaign focused on the Western Hemisphere, where the LAI supported the Nicaraguan resistance led by Augusto César Sandino against the United States military occupation that had begun in 1926 and continued through 1933. The Sandino campaign was particularly important for the LAI's ideological positioning, demonstrating that its anti-imperialism was directed not solely against European colonial powers but against the newer forms of U.S. hemispheric intervention as well — a positioning that aligned the LAI with Latin American anti-imperialist currents and complicated any reading of the organization as a narrowly European-communist project.

A fourth area of activity concerned the Middle East and North Africa, where the LAI engaged with the political aftermath of the Syrian revolt, the consolidation of French rule in the Maghreb, and the continuing British presence in Egypt and Palestine. The LAI's positioning on Palestine — a territory whose mandate, as Chapter 2 noted, was uniquely structured by the Balfour-derived obligation toward a Jewish national home — required navigating between solidarity with Arab nationalist resistance to the implementation of that obligation and a Comintern policy on Zionism that varied across the period.

Underpinning these campaigns was the LAI's press, publication, and propaganda work, coordinated principally from Berlin. The organization published bulletins and periodicals in multiple languages, distributed materials through national sections and affiliated organizations, and participated in the broader Münzenberg media ecosystem that operated through the IAH and related front organizations. The volume and reach of this output were considerable for an organization of the LAI's resources, though they remained substantially dependent on the Comintern-aligned infrastructure that Münzenberg had developed for other purposes.

The structural constraints on the LAI's operational reach were severe. Financial dependence on Comintern resources meant that the organization's autonomy was always conditional and that its program was vulnerable to shifts in Soviet strategic priorities. The geographic dispersal of national sections — necessary to the organization's transnational claim but difficult to coordinate from a single secretariat — meant that activities in different territories were often pursued by sections operating with limited reference to one another. Metropolitan state surveillance and harassment, intensifying through the late 1920s and 1930s, restricted the LAI's freedom of action in many of the European countries where its non-imperial work necessarily had to be conducted. The combination of these constraints meant that the LAI's operational capacity, while genuinely transnational, was never commensurate with the global ambitions articulated at Brussels.

## 3.5 Internal Fractures: The Communist–Non-Communist Divide and the Frankfurt Congress

The unresolved tensions identified in subsection 3.2 fractured the LAI's coalition with remarkable speed once the strategic conditions that had held them in suspension were altered. The decisive shift originated in the Comintern's Sixth World Congress, held in Moscow from July to September 1928, which inaugurated the so-called "Third Period" doctrine. The new line abandoned the united-front strategy of cooperation with non-communist nationalist movements and reclassified social democrats, reformist trade unionists, and bourgeois nationalists as "social fascists" or "national reformists" — that is, as principal adversaries of the proletarian movement rather than as tactical allies. The "class against class" line that emerged from this shift required, in colonial contexts, the construction of communist-led movements oriented against the indigenous nationalist bourgeoisies with whom the united front had previously cultivated relations.

The consequences for the LAI were immediate and severe. Non-communist participants who had joined the Brussels coalition on the understanding that the LAI was a broad anti-imperialist front were now redefined within Comintern doctrine as adversaries whose retention in the organization served, at best, the tactical purpose of detaching their followers from their leadership. The LAI's second congress, held at Frankfurt in 1929, became the principal occasion for the implementation of this redefinition. Non-communist figures faced exclusion or were placed in untenable positions; the British Independent Labour Party affiliates, who had participated significantly in the LAI's metropolitan work, became targets of the new line; and the organization's broader coalitional character began to dissolve into a more strictly Comintern-aligned configuration.

Jawaharlal Nehru's relationship with the LAI offers a particularly instructive case study of these dynamics. Nehru had emerged from Brussels with significant personal investment in the LAI's project and a corresponding ideological evolution that integrated socialist analysis with Indian nationalism in ways that would mark his subsequent political trajectory. The shift to the "class against class" line, however, brought the LAI's evaluation of the Indian National Congress as a "national reformist" bourgeois organization into direct conflict with Nehru's primary political identity as a Congress leader. The trajectory of his subsequent disengagement from the LAI — culminating in his formal expulsion in the early 1930s — reflected the broader fate of the non-communist nationalist participation at Brussels. The personal connections Nehru had formed at Brussels, however, did not dissolve with his disengagement from the institution; they would resurface, more than two decades later, in the diplomatic architecture of Afro-Asian solidarity that culminated at Bandung.

The progressive narrowing of the LAI into a more strictly Comintern-aligned body during the early 1930s had several consequences. Operationally, it weakened the organization by removing the non-communist constituencies whose participation had given the Brussels coalition its breadth and metropolitan respectability. Ideologically, it clarified — at significant institutional cost — the longer-term alternatives within anti-colonial politics: between a communist current that subordinated national liberation to global proletarian revolution and a nationalist current that prioritized the political independence of particular territories over global revolutionary commitments. This clarification, though produced through the destruction of the Brussels coalition, would prove durable. The post-1945 Third World project, examined in Chapter 8, would construct its political identity precisely along the second of these axes — affirming national sovereignty and economic emancipation as primary categories — while engaging tactically and selectively with both Cold War blocs.

## 3.6 Confrontation with the League of Nations and the Politics of Counter-Internationalism

The LAI's relationship to the Geneva-based internationalism examined in Chapter 2 was one of deliberate and public confrontation. The organization defined itself, at Brussels and in its subsequent publications, against the official multilateral order, and its critique articulated several specific positions that bear close examination for what they reveal about the implicit dialogue between the two interwar tracks of internationalization.

First, the LAI rejected the Mandates System as a legal innovation that legitimized rather than constrained continued imperial rule. In the LAI's framing, the substitution of "mandate" for "colony" amounted to a rhetorical evasion that left the substantive relationship between metropole and territory essentially unchanged while supplying it with the additional moral authority of multilateral endorsement. The administrative practices examined in Chapter 2 — the Class C territories governed "as integral portions" of the mandatory, the Class B territories administered under the same colonial frameworks as adjacent possessions, the Class A territories whose "provisional recognition" did not in practice produce substantive independence — all furnished the LAI with empirical material confirming this critique. The Permanent Mandates Commission, which Chapter 2 examined in detail, appeared in the LAI's analysis less as a constraining oversight body than as a multilateral instrument for the legitimation of imperial administration.

Second, the LAI rejected the civilizational hierarchies that organized Article 22 and the broader League of Nations vocabulary. The differentiation of "peoples not yet able to stand by themselves" into A, B, and C categories — examined in subsection 2.2 — appeared in the LAI's framework not as a graduated tutelary regime responsive to differing developmental conditions but as a racialized hierarchy whose underlying assumptions were incompatible with the principle of universal national self-determination that the LAI affirmed.

Third, the LAI constructed an alternative international publicity for colonial grievances outside the petition procedures examined in subsection 2.6. Where the Permanent Mandates Commission's procedure required that petitions be transmitted through the mandatory power, restricted oral testimony from colonized petitioners, and operated within a framework of presumed mandatory good faith, the LAI's publicity work admitted colonized voices directly and amplified them without mediation. The political effects of this difference were significant: a grievance routed through the Geneva petition procedure entered an institutional record under conditions controlled by the mandatory, while a grievance amplified through the LAI's press and convening capacities entered international debate under the framing of the anti-imperialist movement itself.

The relationship between the LAI and the League of Nations was not, however, simply one of mutual exclusion. The LAI's existence and rhetoric exerted indirect pressure on the official international system in at least two ways. It raised the political costs of imperial conduct by ensuring that abuses would receive international publicity independent of the procedural constraints that governed Geneva, and it supplied nationalist movements with internationalist vocabularies — "imperialism," "self-determination of peoples," "national independence" — that they could and did redeploy within the official forums when occasion permitted. Petitioners to the Permanent Mandates Commission frequently invoked the very legal categories the Covenant had inscribed (the "sacred trust," non-annexation, the safeguards against abuse), as Chapter 2 noted; LAI participation and adjacent transnational activism supplied them additionally with the broader political vocabulary in which their particular grievances could be theorized as instances of a global phenomenon. The two tracks of interwar internationalization were thus not isolated; they operated in implicit dialogue, and the conceptual resources generated within each circulated, in modified form, through the other.

## 3.7 Decline, Suppression, and the End of the Interwar LAI

The trajectory of the LAI through the early and mid-1930s is a story of compounding adversity. The internal fractures examined in subsection 3.5 had already weakened the organization significantly by the time external pressures intensified. The Nazi seizure of power in January 1933 produced the most immediate and severe blow: the LAI's Berlin headquarters became untenable, the organization's German infrastructure — including Münzenberg's broader Comintern-front network — was disrupted by Gestapo action, and the operational coordination that the Berlin secretariat had previously sustained could not be reconstructed at comparable scale elsewhere. The headquarters relocated, but the relocation marked a decisive contraction of the organization's effective capacity.

The broader political environment of metropolitan state repression intensified through the same period. The colonial intelligence services of the principal imperial powers — Britain's Indian Political Intelligence, the French Sûreté générale, and their counterparts — had monitored the LAI from its founding and had developed substantial capacities for surveilling its national sections, harassing its activists, and restricting the circulation of its publications. The intensification of this repression, combined with the general European political crisis of the mid-1930s, narrowed the operational space within which the LAI could function.

The Comintern's strategic recalibration in the mid-1930s — the shift to the Popular Front strategy formalized at the Seventh World Congress in 1935 — fundamentally altered the political context within which the LAI had operated. The Popular Front prioritized cooperation with social democrats, liberals, and other anti-fascist forces against the immediate Nazi and fascist threats, and it correspondingly de-emphasized the explicit anti-imperialism that had defined the LAI's mission. From the perspective of Soviet foreign policy, increasingly preoccupied with the prospect of war and seeking accommodation with the imperial powers (Britain and France) whose colonial holdings the LAI had been organized to contest, the maintenance of a high-profile anti-imperialist front became strategically counterproductive. The dissolution of the LAI in 1937 was thus the institutional expression of these converging pressures: the Comintern's shifted priorities, the external repression that had eroded operational viability, and the internal coalition that had already fragmented under the strain of the Third Period turn.

The institutional failure of the LAI in 1937 should not, however, be assimilated to a judgment of broader political failure. The organization had accomplished, in the decade of its existence, what no previous transnational anti-colonial body had accomplished: the convening of a genuinely global coalition, the elaboration of a common political vocabulary across previously separate regional currents, and the construction of personal and intellectual networks that proved more durable than the institution that had generated them. The assessment of these legacies is the subject of subsection 3.8.

## 3.8 Longer-Term Legacies: From Brussels to Bandung and the Third World Project

The bequest of the LAI to subsequent anti-colonial and Third World politics can be assessed along three intersecting dimensions: personal trajectories, organizational templates, and discursive innovations.

The personal trajectories are the most directly traceable. Jawaharlal Nehru's participation at Brussels has been widely identified as a formative moment in his political education, and the ideological synthesis that he developed in subsequent decades — integrating socialist economic analysis with Indian nationalist politics and with an internationalist orientation toward Asian and African solidarity — drew substantially on the conceptual and personal resources that Brussels had supplied. After Indian independence in 1947, as the first Prime Minister of independent India and as a principal architect of Indian foreign policy, Nehru carried this orientation into the diplomatic construction of Afro-Asian solidarity that culminated in the Bandung Conference of April 1955, the subject of detailed examination in Chapter 8. Mohammad Hatta's trajectory — from the Perhimpoenan Indonesia in the Netherlands and the Brussels participation, through the leadership of the Indonesian independence movement, to service as Vice-President of independent Indonesia and as a Bandung co-host alongside Sukarno — followed a parallel arc. Other Brussels participants whose subsequent political careers carried elements of the LAI legacy into post-1945 politics included figures across multiple regional contexts whose biographies the present chapter cannot exhaustively reconstruct but whose collective trajectory amounts to a substantial transmission of interwar anti-colonial internationalism into the post-war Third World project.

The organizational templates that the LAI bequeathed concerned the practice of transnational anti-colonial convening: the assembly of a heterogeneous coalition around shared political principles, the use of conference and congress formats to construct mutual recognition among previously separate movements, the coordination of publicity campaigns across multiple national contexts, and the construction of an alternative international publicity outside the procedures of official multilateral institutions. These templates would be reactivated, in modified form, at Bandung in 1955, at the founding of the Non-Aligned Movement at Belgrade in 1961, and at the convening of the Group of 77 around UNCTAD I in 1964 — all of which are examined in Chapter 8. The Bandung Conference in particular reproduced the LAI's pattern of convening a geographically diverse anti-colonial coalition around shared political principles while explicitly distinguishing itself from the bloc politics of the Cold War — though now from the position of newly sovereign states rather than insurgent movements, and now within rather than against the post-1945 international system.

The discursive innovations are the most consequential and the most diffuse. The LAI's central conceptual claim — that imperialism was a global system whose contestation required correspondingly global coordination — provided the architecture within which the post-1945 Third World project would think its own identity. The linkage of formal political independence to economic emancipation, which the LAI had articulated in its critique of the Mandates System and in its broader political program, anticipated the central claim of the New International Economic Order: that the legal achievement of sovereignty did not exhaust the requirements of substantive decolonization, and that economic structures inherited from the colonial era required deliberate transformation if formal independence was to acquire material content. The assertion of a self-organized anti-colonial international voice — independent of, and capable of contesting, the framing operations of the metropolitan international system — anticipated the Group of 77's strategy within the United Nations and its specialized agencies, which Chapter 8 examines in detail.

The continuities between the LAI and the post-1945 Third World project should not, however, be overstated. The post-1945 movement operated from the institutional position of independent states whose sovereignty was internationally recognized, whose representation in the United Nations General Assembly accumulated quantitatively across the period examined in Chapter 7, and whose tactical capacities included the use of voting majorities in multilateral forums that no interwar anti-colonial movement could approach. The Non-Aligned Movement and the Group of 77 distinguished themselves from communist internationalism in ways that the LAI's hybrid coalitional architecture had not, and they engaged with both Cold War blocs from a position of declared autonomy rather than aligning with the Soviet-led current. The discursive innovations the LAI had pioneered were thus transmitted into a transformed political context whose institutional and strategic logics differed substantially from the interwar conjuncture.

The chapter's argument can now be brought into focus. The LAI was a short-lived, institutionally fragile, and substantially defeated organization. Measured against the global ambitions articulated at Brussels in February 1927, its operational accomplishments were modest, its coalition fractured rapidly, and its dissolution in 1937 represented a clear failure of the institutional form it had attempted. Measured, however, as a node within the longer durée of anti-colonial internationalism, the LAI's significance is substantial. It convened the most geographically diverse anti-colonial coalition of the interwar period; it elaborated the discursive infrastructure through which previously separate regional struggles could be theorized as articulations of a single global phenomenon; it constructed personal and intellectual networks that proved more durable than the institution itself; and it bequeathed organizational templates and discursive resources that resurfaced, in modified form, in the post-1945 Third World project. The supervisory track of interwar internationalization examined in Chapter 2 and the counter-mobilizational track examined in this chapter operated, throughout the period, in implicit dialogue: each generated conceptual and political resources that circulated, in modified form, through the other; each registered the interwar internationalization of colonial affairs from a different institutional vantage; and each conditioned, in distinct ways, the institutional and ideological transformations of the post-1945 era that Chapter 4 will now begin to examine, beginning with the transition from the League's Mandates System to the United Nations' Trusteeship arrangements codified at San Francisco in 1945.

# 4 From Mandates to Trusteeship: The Founding of the United Nations and the Codification of Decolonization Norms

Chapter 3 concluded by tracing how the supervisory and counter-mobilizational tracks of interwar internationalization — the Permanent Mandates Commission at Geneva and the League Against Imperialism at Brussels — generated distinct but mutually conditioning resources that fed into the post-1945 reconstruction of world order. This chapter takes up that reconstruction itself. It examines how, between the proclamation of the Atlantic Charter in August 1941 and the entry into force of the United Nations Charter on 24 October 1945, the Allied Powers translated wartime declarations on self-determination into the institutional architecture that would govern colonial affairs for the next two decades. The result was the constellation of provisions distributed across Chapters XI, XII, and XIII of the Charter — the Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories, the International Trusteeship System, and the Trusteeship Council — which together replaced the League's Mandates System with a regime that was at once more ambitious in its normative commitments and more cautiously framed in its operational obligations.

The argument advanced in what follows treats the 1945 settlement neither as the inevitable fulfillment of an emergent global norm of self-determination nor as a mere repackaging of imperial prerogatives. Both characterizations capture elements of the historical record, but each understates the contingency of the wartime negotiations and the contested character of the compromises they produced. The Charter's colonial provisions were the product of a specific diplomatic conjuncture in which American Wilsonian sentiment, British imperial defensiveness, Soviet rhetorical anti-colonialism, and the strategic preoccupations of the United States military establishment all left distinct marks on the final text. The institutional framework they yielded was a layered compromise: it inherited the supervisory template developed at Geneva, strengthened it in important respects (most notably through the explicit telos of self-government or independence and the procedural innovations of direct petitions and visiting missions), and simultaneously left intact the broader prerogatives of colonial powers and the deferral of contested questions about timetables and criteria for self-governance. It was, in the terms developed in the introduction to this report, a settlement whose universalist language would prove appropriable in ways that its principal architects did not anticipate.

## 4.1 Wartime Debates over the Future of Empire: The Atlantic Charter and Allied Diplomacy

The wartime origin of postwar decolonization norms is most plausibly located not in any single decision but in the cumulative diplomatic process that began with the meeting of Franklin D. Roosevelt and Winston Churchill in Placentia Bay, Newfoundland, on 9–10 August 1941, and that extended through the Declaration by United Nations of 1 January 1942, the Moscow Declaration of October 1943, the Yalta Conference of February 1945, and the San Francisco Conference of April–June 1945. Throughout this process, the Atlantic Charter occupied a peculiar position: it was at once a non-binding joint statement of war aims with eight articulated principles, an instrument that bound — through the Declaration by United Nations — the broader Allied coalition to those principles in the war against the Axis, and a rhetorical resource whose subsequent appropriation by colonized peoples far exceeded the scope its principal authors had envisaged.[^4][^5]

The Charter was the product of a meeting whose primary purpose was not the elaboration of self-determination but the calibration of Anglo-American wartime cooperation. By mid-1941, Britain had survived the German aerial onslaught of the previous autumn and had been joined as a belligerent by the Soviet Union following the German invasion on 22 June 1941, while the United States, formally still a non-belligerent, was extending material support through the Lend-Lease Act of March 1941 and was contemplating the longer-term geopolitical implications of an Axis victory.[^4][^5] Churchill's principal hope at the conference was to secure American entry into the war, or failing that, an increased flow of military aid; Roosevelt's principal hope was to issue a public statement of war aims that would distinguish the values of the Allied democracies from those of the Axis Powers and that might shift American public opinion toward intervention.[^4][^5] Neither leader achieved his primary objective in the form anticipated. The Charter that emerged on 14 August 1941 contained eight points, of which two — the renunciation of territorial changes "contrary to the wishes of the peoples concerned" and the affirmation of "the right of all peoples to choose the form of government under which they will live" — directly engaged the principle of self-determination.[^4][^5]

The drafting history of these self-determination clauses reveals the layered ambiguity that would define their subsequent career. Roosevelt's Wilsonian sympathies on self-determination were known to the British, and the draft prepared by the British permanent undersecretary Alexander Cadogan, subsequently revised by Churchill, included statements endorsing the concept; Roosevelt did not press the point against Churchill's clear view that "the British Commonwealth's commitment to eventual colonial self-government (if not independence) made the principle inapplicable to the British Empire."[^4] The points that proved most controversial in the drafting concerned not self-determination but the postwar trade regime — specifically the tension between Roosevelt's commitment to free trade and Britain's system of Imperial Preference — and the framing of postwar security in language that would not alarm American isolationists.[^4][^5] On the textual surface, the self-determination provisions thus passed into the Charter as principles to which neither side regarded itself as forced to make substantive concessions; their open-ended formulation made them politically tolerable precisely because each party understood them through its own preferred construction.

The divergence of those constructions became apparent almost immediately and structured the subsequent inter-Allied diplomacy on colonial questions. The British position, articulated repeatedly through the war by Churchill and reinforced in Parliament, held that the Charter's self-determination provisions applied principally to "the European countries overrun and occupied by the Nazis" and did not extend to the British Empire.[^4] The American position evolved in a contrary direction: although Roosevelt did not press Churchill at Placentia Bay, the State Department over the course of the war showed "a growing willingness to see the concept of self-determination as applicable outside Europe — certainly to the colonies of the Axis Powers and possibly to those of the Allies as well."[^4] The Soviet position combined a rhetorical embrace of self-determination "everywhere" with what an authoritative summary identifies as "clear expectation of beneficial territorial rearrangements in Eastern Europe and Asia" — a positioning that aligned Moscow rhetorically with the more expansive American reading while leaving its actual policy in the European theater consistent with patterns of territorial revision that Wilsonian self-determination would have problematized.[^4]

The bridge from these divergent constructions to the broader Allied coalition was the Declaration by United Nations of 1 January 1942, in which the signatories — twenty-six states initially, eventually doubling — agreed both to the principles of the Atlantic Charter and to refraining from a separate peace with the enemy.[^4] The Declaration converted what had been a bilateral Anglo-American statement into a multilateral commitment binding the wartime coalition, and in doing so it inscribed the Charter's self-determination language into the foundational normative architecture of the war effort itself. The Yalta Conference of February 1945 reaffirmed this commitment through the Declaration on Liberated Europe, which called for free elections and the application of Atlantic Charter principles in the territories liberated from Axis occupation.[^4] The juridical effect of these reaffirmations was limited; the political effect was substantial, in that each successive endorsement made it more difficult for any of the principal Allies to disavow the principle even as its practical application remained contested.

The most consequential dimension of the Charter, however, was its appropriation by audiences whose interpretations its principal authors had not anticipated. As a summary judgment in the historiographical literature observes, the Charter "ultimately did serve as an inspiration for colonial subjects throughout the Third World, from Algeria to Vietnam, as they fought for independence."[^5] The Viet Minh in Indochina, the Congress Party in India, and a wide range of African and Middle Eastern nationalist movements invoked the Charter as moral and rhetorical authority against their colonial overlords, deploying its language to argue that the war's universalist commitments had to extend, in consistency, to the dependent territories of the victorious Allies.[^4] The argument did not produce immediate concessions; the practical resistance of colonial powers to extending Atlantic Charter principles to their own possessions remained intact through the war. But the Charter established a discursive baseline against which colonial conduct could thereafter be evaluated and rhetorically contested — a baseline whose subsequent codification in the UN Charter and progressive radicalization through General Assembly practice constitute the substance of the chapters that follow.

Several features of the wartime diplomatic process deserve emphasis as preparation for the institutional negotiations examined in the next subchapter. First, the absence of a comprehensive postwar treaty — in contrast to the Versailles settlement that had produced both the League Covenant and the territorial reorganization of Europe — meant that the codification of decolonization norms would proceed not through a single peace settlement but through the construction of the new international organization itself, with the Charter assuming the dual function of constituting the organization and articulating the principles by which it would conduct its work.[^4] Second, the wartime weakening of the European colonial powers, particularly through the loss of South-East Asian territories to Japanese occupation between 1941 and 1942, materially altered the postwar balance of capacities even as the formal political claims of the imperial powers to their pre-war possessions were not relinquished. Third, the Cold War cleavage that would shape post-1945 diplomacy was already incipient by the time of Yalta and would, as the next subchapter examines, condition the contested negotiations on dependent territories at San Francisco. The institutional design produced at San Francisco was thus shaped by all three of these features: by the universalist rhetorical commitments inherited from the Charter and Declaration, by the strategic recalibrations of the wartime power balance, and by the early signals of the bipolar political configuration that would dominate the subsequent decade.

## 4.2 From Dumbarton Oaks to San Francisco: Negotiating the Charter's Colonial Provisions

The institutional translation of the wartime universalist commitments into the operative provisions of the UN Charter passed through three principal negotiating venues: the Dumbarton Oaks Conversations of August–October 1944, the Yalta Conference of February 1945, and the San Francisco Conference (United Nations Conference on International Organization, UNCIO) of April–June 1945. Each of these venues produced a distinct increment of the eventual settlement; each was structured by a different configuration of bureaucratic, inter-Allied, and broader coalitional pressures; and each illuminates the contested character of the compromises that would yield Chapters XI, XII, and XIII of the Charter.

The Dumbarton Oaks Conversations, held at the mansion of that name in Georgetown, Washington, from 21 August to 7 October 1944, brought together representatives of China, the Soviet Union, the United States, and the United Kingdom for the purpose of formulating proposals for the postwar international organization.[^6] The Dumbarton Oaks Proposals (Proposals for the Establishment of a General International Organization) addressed the principal architectural questions of the future organization — its purposes, principles, membership, principal organs, and the relationship between the Security Council and the General Assembly — but on two critical issues they did not produce agreement: the voting procedure of the Security Council and the membership provisions for the constituent republics of the Soviet Union.[^6] The proposals also notably did not furnish a comprehensive blueprint for the colonial provisions of the future Charter. The trusteeship arrangements were among the issues "considered" at Dumbarton Oaks but not resolved there.[^7] The voting and trusteeship questions were both deferred for further consideration, the former to Yalta and the latter to a combination of Yalta and the San Francisco Conference itself.[^6]

The deferral of the trusteeship question at Dumbarton Oaks reflected genuine inter-Allied disagreement and substantial intra-American bureaucratic tension. The documentary record of the US delegation's preparation for the San Francisco Conference, preserved in the *Foreign Relations of the United States* volume for 1945, exposes the depth of the interdepartmental contests within the American government in the months following Dumbarton Oaks.[^8] In January 1945, in preparation for the Yalta Conference, the Department of State produced briefing materials and policy papers addressing trusteeship questions, including a memorandum from the Secretary of War (Stimson) of 23 January 1945 setting out the views of the War Department on international trusteeship.[^8] The State Department for its part had begun, by the same period, organizing systematic consultations with Latin American republics and with other interested governments on the Dumbarton Oaks Proposals and the dependent-territories questions that those Proposals had left open.[^8]

The configuration of interdepartmental positions within the American government can be reconstructed in some detail from the published record. The principal cleavage operated between, on the one hand, the State Department's interest in articulating a trusteeship system whose provisions and procedures would inscribe the universalist commitments of the Atlantic Charter and the Declaration by United Nations into binding institutional architecture, and, on the other hand, the War and Navy Departments' strategic preoccupation with retaining United States control over Pacific island bases captured from Japan during the Pacific War. A memorandum of the Chairman of the State–War–Navy Coordinating Committee (Dunn) to the Secretary of State of 26 February 1945 conveyed that "Joint Chiefs of Staff express no objection to proposed discussion of international trusteeships under certain specified conditions," but the conditions themselves embodied the military's insistence on preserving strategic prerogatives.[^8] By 5 April 1945, the Secretary of the Interior (Ickes) was transmitting a memorandum on the international trusteeship problem; on 7 April, the Chief of the Division of Dependent Areas (Gerig) recorded "differing views of the Navy Department, the War Department, the Interior Department, and the State Department" on a draft memorandum on trusteeship.[^8] The Secretary of State submitted papers to the President on 9 April 1945 reporting on international trusteeship problems, and a meeting of the Secretaries of State, War, and Navy with President Truman on 18 April yielded an approved policy directive on trusteeship — a directive whose substance reflected the compromise that had to be negotiated within the American government itself before any common American position could be projected at San Francisco.[^8]

The compromise that emerged from this interdepartmental process was the architecture that subsequently shaped the United States' draft proposals at San Francisco. The American delegation arrived at San Francisco with a draft trusteeship structure that distinguished between ordinary (non-strategic) trust territories, whose supervision would be conducted by the Trusteeship Council under the authority of the General Assembly, and "strategic" trust territories, whose supervision would fall under the authority of the Security Council — a category designed to accommodate the War and Navy Departments' insistence on preserving United States control over the Pacific island bases captured from Japan. A memorandum of conversation by the Adviser on Caribbean Affairs (Taussig) of 15 March 1945 recorded an extensive discussion with President Roosevelt "regarding trusteeship, Arabia, Caribbean bases, East Asian peoples, Indo-China, and New Caledonia" — a conversation that ranged across virtually the full geographical scope of the issues that the trusteeship question would have to address.[^8]

The Yalta Conference of February 1945 made two contributions to the colonial settlement. First, it resolved the Security Council voting question that had been deferred from Dumbarton Oaks, producing the formula on the unanimity of permanent members (the "veto") that would shape the entire subsequent character of the UN system. Second, it produced an agreement among the Big Three on the framework of the trusteeship system, providing for a system that would replace the League of Nations Mandates System and that would receive its detailed elaboration at the San Francisco Conference.[^6] As the Encyclopædia Britannica's summary records, the Yalta Conference "resulted in the proposal of a trusteeship system under the new agency to take the place of the League of Nations mandate system."[^6] The framework agreed at Yalta was deliberately general; it did not address the specific scope of trusteeship, the procedures of supervision, or the role of administering powers, all of which were left for negotiation at San Francisco. A telegram from the Acting Secretary of State of 11 February 1945 conveyed to the Ambassador in France the substance of the Yalta communiqué, which included "proposed consultation of the five inviting Governments, prior to the conference, on trusteeship."[^8]

Pre-conference five-power consultations on trusteeship were the subject of significant Anglo-American negotiation in the weeks following Yalta. A memorandum by Pasvolsky of 13 January 1945 had already addressed "forthcoming conversations with Colonel Stanley, British Secretary of State for the Colonies," and outlined US and British views on dependent areas; the British, on this account, remained "as reluctant as before to embrace a definition of self-determination that would apply to their empire."[^4][^8] The American negotiating position, as it evolved in the spring of 1945, had to balance the bureaucratic compromise on strategic trusts against the State Department's broader normative ambitions for the trusteeship regime. A memorandum prepared by the Interdepartmental Committee on Dependent Areas of 17 March 1945 produced a "Draft statement on international trusteeship, submitted to the Secretary of State's Staff Committee," which formed the basis of the working position carried forward through subsequent deliberations.[^8]

The San Francisco Conference (UNCIO) opened on 25 April 1945 and concluded on 26 June 1945 with the signing of the Charter. The Dumbarton Oaks Proposals, as modified by Yalta, constituted the agenda of the Conference, and proposed amendments were to be submitted by midnight on 4 May 1945.[^8] The conference allocated the trusteeship questions principally to Committee II/4 (the Trusteeship Committee), which addressed both the trusteeship system itself and the broader question of non-self-governing territories. The American delegation's draft proposals for the trusteeship chapters were approved by the delegation on 26 April 1945 as a basis for discussion in the conference, and a substantial draft text (which would become the basis of Chapters XII and XIII) was tabled for consideration.[^8]

Several features of the San Francisco negotiations on trusteeship deserve emphasis. First, the participation of representatives of dependent peoples and of the Arab states injected positions into the conference that were not directly accommodated within the framework agreed by the Big Five. The minutes of the United States Delegation record amendments proposed by the Arab League and by Iraq involving Palestine and the question of the disposition of the Class A mandates; these amendments were considered alongside Egyptian amendments on the termination and transfer of trust areas.[^8] Second, the question of whether "independence" should be included as an explicit objective of the trusteeship system was sharply contested. The minutes of the United States Delegation of 17 May 1945 recorded the inclusion of "'independence' as a goal" in the discussion of Article 76(b) of the future Trusteeship chapter, and on 18 May the question of "Trusteeship: 'independence' for dependent peoples as a goal" was again on the delegation's agenda.[^8] The inclusion of "independence" as an explicit alternative to "self-government" in Article 76(b) — alongside "self-government" — was contested by the British delegation, which preferred the formulation "self-government" alone, on the ground that "self-government" could accommodate forms of association short of full independence.[^4] The Soviet delegation, by contrast, pressed for the explicit inclusion of independence, consistent with its rhetorical anti-colonialism. The compromise text — Article 76(b)'s reference to "progressive development towards self-government or independence as may be appropriate to the particular circumstances of each territory and its peoples and the freely expressed wishes of the peoples concerned" — incorporated both terms while qualifying the trajectory with the "as may be appropriate" clause that preserved interpretive flexibility for administering powers. The Acting Secretary of State's telegram of 8 May 1945 conveyed "Molotov's press statement on self-government for dependent peoples," indicating the importance attached by the Soviet side to publicizing its position on the question.[^8]

Third, the negotiation of what would become Article 80 — the "conservatory clause" preserving existing rights and the terms of existing international instruments until they were superseded by trusteeship agreements — was an important element of the package that secured British and Australian acceptance of the broader Trusteeship arrangement. The minutes of the US Delegation of 5 June 1945 recorded consideration of "art. 80(1), the 'conservatory clause,'" together with the objectives of Article 76(b) on trusteeship.[^8] On 9 June, a memorandum of conversation by Gerig addressed "Charter article 80(2), Trusteeship: delay or postponement of agreements, and Soviet eligibility as a potential administering authority"; the Soviet interest in being eligible as a trustee administering authority was conveyed by Gromyko to Stettinius in a communication of 20 June 1945, and Stettinius's reply of 23 June addressed "eligibility of Soviet Union as potential administering authority."[^8]

Fourth, the language used to articulate the relationship between self-determination and the trusteeship and non-self-governing territories provisions was carefully calibrated. The minutes of the US Delegation of 9 June 1945 recorded consideration of "Charter articles 1(2), 76(b), and 55, use of the language of the Atlantic Charter with respect to self-determination" — a deliberate textual linkage between the Atlantic Charter's principles and the Charter provisions on dependent territories.[^8] This linkage embedded the wartime universalist commitments traced in the previous subchapter into the Charter's articulation of the purposes of the United Nations (Article 1(2) on developing friendly relations among nations based on respect for the principle of equal rights and self-determination of peoples), the objectives of the Trusteeship System (Article 76(b)), and the objectives of international economic and social cooperation (Article 55).

Finally, the broader question of Chapter XI's Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories — addressed in detail in subchapter 4.5 — was the subject of intense San Francisco negotiation that ran on a partly separate track from the trusteeship negotiations. The Chapter XI provisions emerged from proposals advanced principally by Australia, with significant participation by New Zealand and the United States, and they represented the most far-reaching textual extension of international interest to colonial territories that was achievable at San Francisco. Their negotiation will be examined in subchapter 4.5; here it suffices to note that the eventual settlement on colonial questions at San Francisco comprised a layered architecture in which the Trusteeship provisions (Chapters XII and XIII) addressed the narrower category of formal trust territories, the Chapter XI Declaration addressed the much broader category of all non-self-governing territories administered by member states, and the Atlantic-Charter–derived language of Article 1(2) and Article 55 provided the connective normative tissue across the whole.

The complete Charter, including the Statute of the International Court of Justice, was certified by the Secretary of State to President Truman on 26 June 1945, and the Charter entered into force on 24 October 1945.[^8] The Preparatory Commission of the United Nations, which conducted its work in London through the autumn and winter of 1945–46, addressed the practical questions of establishing the organization, including the question of the permanent location of headquarters (eventually decided in favor of the United States), the recruitment of the Secretariat, and the preparation of the rules of procedure of the principal organs.[^8] The General Assembly's first session convened in January 1946; the Trusteeship Council was constituted later in 1946 following the conclusion of the first trusteeship agreements.

## 4.3 Comparative Legal Architecture: Mandates and Trusteeship

The institutional outputs of the Dumbarton Oaks–Yalta–San Francisco negotiating sequence — Chapters XII and XIII of the UN Charter, together with the Declaration of Chapter XI — must now be set in systematic comparison with the League of Nations Mandates System examined in Chapter 2. The comparison is essential for understanding the precise character of the 1945 settlement as both continuation and rupture. It can be organized across five dimensions: the underlying normative formulas; the categorization of territories; the supervisory institutions; the procedural mechanisms; and the legal status of administering powers.

The following table summarizes the principal comparative features:

| Dimension | League of Nations Mandates System (1919–1946) | UN Trusteeship System (1945–1994) |
|---|---|---|
| Normative formula | "Sacred trust of civilization" (Covenant Art. 22, §1); "peoples not yet able to stand by themselves under the strenuous conditions of the modern world" | "Progressive development towards self-government or independence as may be appropriate" (Charter Art. 76(b)); "freely expressed wishes of the peoples concerned" |
| Categorization | Class A (Middle East, "provisionally recognized" as independent), Class B (Central Africa, direct administration with safeguards), Class C (Pacific/South West Africa, administered "as integral portions" of mandatory) | Ordinary (non-strategic) trust territories under General Assembly authority; "strategic" trust territories under Security Council authority |
| Supervisory body | Permanent Mandates Commission (10–11 expert members in personal capacity, advisory to League Council) | Trusteeship Council (state representatives, parity between administering and non-administering members, principal organ of UN) |
| Procedural mechanisms | Annual reports from mandatories; written petitions through mandatory power; private examination of mandatory representatives | Annual reports; written and oral petitions, including direct from petitioners; periodic visiting missions to trust territories |
| Trajectory / telos | Explicit only for Class A ("until such time as they are able to stand alone"); deferred indefinitely for Classes B and C | Explicit for all trust territories: "self-government or independence" as objective of every trusteeship |
| Legal status of administering powers | "Mandatories on behalf of the League" — sui generis tutelage, non-annexation | Administering authorities under trusteeship agreements; territorial status under international supervision |
| Universal scope | Limited to former Ottoman and German territories | Extended through Chapter XI Declaration to all non-self-governing territories administered by member states |

The interpretive analysis underlying this comparison can now be developed.

**Normative formula and teleology.** The most significant rupture between the two regimes lies in the explicit articulation of the goal of self-government or independence under Article 76(b) of the UN Charter. Article 22 of the Covenant, as Chapter 2 examined, articulated the formula of the "sacred trust of civilization" and provided that the "tutelage" of peoples "not yet able to stand by themselves" should be exercised by "advanced nations." This formulation was simultaneously committed to a normative standard (the well-being and development of the peoples concerned) and was inscribed within an unmistakably civilizational vocabulary. The Article 22 formula did not, except for Class A, articulate an explicit temporal telos of independence; the Covenant provided that Class A territories would receive administrative advice and assistance "until such time as they are able to stand alone," but Class B and Class C territories were committed to no comparable trajectory. Article 76(b) of the UN Charter, by contrast, established as a basic objective of the Trusteeship System "the progressive development towards self-government or independence as may be appropriate to the particular circumstances of each territory and its peoples and the freely expressed wishes of the peoples concerned." The textual achievement is double: it inscribes independence as an explicit alternative goal (not merely deferred until the territory is "able to stand alone"), and it inscribes the "freely expressed wishes of the peoples concerned" as a substantive criterion for the determination of trajectory. The qualifying language ("as may be appropriate") preserved interpretive flexibility for administering powers, but the textual basis for invoking the goal of independence against administering-power resistance was now firmly established.

**Categorization.** The League's tripartite classification (Class A, B, C), examined in detail in Chapter 2, articulated a graduated tutelary regime structured by racialized assumptions about developmental readiness. The UN Trusteeship System's categorical distinction was different in character: it bifurcated trust territories into ordinary (non-strategic) and strategic categories, with the latter defined by the strategic significance of the territory (in practice, for Pacific island bases) and placed under the supervisory authority of the Security Council rather than the General Assembly. The bifurcation thus operated on the basis of strategic-military rather than civilizational criteria, even as the substantive supervision of trust territories under either authority continued to invoke developmental categories in practice. The categorical change reflected the negotiating compromise within the American government identified in subchapter 4.2: the strategic-trust category was the institutional expression of the War and Navy Departments' insistence on preserving United States control over Pacific island bases captured from Japan, channeled through a category in which Security Council supervision (with the permanent members' veto) effectively insulated the administering authority from outvoting by a General Assembly anti-colonial majority. The Pacific Islands trust territory (the Trust Territory of the Pacific Islands, administered by the United States) was the principal — and effectively the only — territory placed under the strategic category.

**Supervisory institutions.** The transition from the Permanent Mandates Commission to the Trusteeship Council marked a fundamental institutional restructuring. The PMC, as Chapter 2 examined, was composed of expert members appointed in their personal capacities, served without term limits, and was advisory to the League Council; despite Pedersen's identification of structural sources of its capacity for independent action, it remained an advisory body whose members were predominantly drawn from imperial establishments. The Trusteeship Council, by contrast, was constituted by Chapter XIII of the Charter (Articles 86–91) as a principal organ of the United Nations, composed of state representatives in three categories: states administering trust territories; the permanent members of the Security Council that did not administer trust territories; and an equal number of additional states elected by the General Assembly to balance the administering states. The result was a designed parity between administering and non-administering members — a deliberate departure from the PMC composition, in which the mandatory powers' influence was numerically more constrained but politically and informationally substantial. Voting in the Trusteeship Council was by simple majority, on the model of the General Assembly. The Council reported to the General Assembly for ordinary trust territories and to the Security Council for strategic trust territories; this differentiation in reporting destinations was the institutional consequence of the strategic/ordinary distinction.

**Procedural mechanisms.** The procedural innovations of the Trusteeship System constituted, in important respects, the most consequential operational advances over the League precedent. The PMC's procedure, as examined in Chapter 2, required that petitions from inhabitants of mandated territories be transmitted through the mandatory power, restricted oral testimony from petitioners in Geneva to narrow circumstances, and operated within a framework that gave mandatory representatives substantial procedural advantage. The Trusteeship System retained the practice of annual reports — Article 88 of the Charter requires the Trusteeship Council to formulate a questionnaire on the political, economic, social, and educational advancement of the inhabitants of each trust territory, and each administering authority is to make an annual report to the General Assembly based on such a questionnaire — but it added two procedural innovations of substantial significance. First, Article 87 of the Charter provided that the General Assembly and the Trusteeship Council may, in carrying out their functions, "accept petitions and examine them in consultation with the administering authority"; the Council's working procedures developed this provision into a practice of receiving petitions directly from inhabitants of trust territories without requiring transmittal through the administering authority, and of admitting oral hearings of petitioners before the Council itself. Second, Article 87 further provided for "periodic visits to the respective trust territories at times agreed upon with the administering authority"; visiting missions, composed of Council members and conducting on-territory observation and reception of petitions, became a regular feature of trusteeship supervision and produced documentary records that fed into the Council's substantive examination of administering-power conduct.

These procedural innovations — direct petitions, oral hearings of petitioners, and visiting missions — addressed precisely the most significant procedural weaknesses of the League regime identified in Chapter 2. The direct admission of colonized voices to the supervisory process, without the mediation of the administering power, substantially strengthened the channel through which colonial grievances could enter the international record. The visiting missions provided the Council with independent informational access to trust territories, reducing — though not eliminating — the dependence on administering-power-supplied information that had characterized the PMC's working environment. The combination of these procedural innovations made the Trusteeship Council a more politically active and substantively engaged supervisory body than the PMC had been, even within the constraints imposed by the broader configuration of power in the UN system.

**Legal status of administering powers.** The principle of non-annexation, which Chapter 2 identified as the most important legal innovation of the League Mandates System, was preserved and extended in the Trusteeship System. Article 81 of the Charter required that each trusteeship agreement specify the terms under which the trust territory will be administered and designate the authority that will exercise the administration. The administering authority — typically a member state, although the Charter contemplated the possibility of the United Nations itself serving as administering authority — held its position under the trusteeship agreement, not as the sovereign of the territory. The continuing distinction between sovereignty and administration meant that trust territories remained internationally supervised entities whose ultimate disposition (whether through independence, free association, or integration with another state) was a matter of international rather than purely metropolitan concern. This continuity with the Mandates System's non-annexation principle was politically and legally consequential: it preserved the structural opening through which trust territories could move toward independence under international supervision rather than through unilateral metropolitan decision.

**Universal scope.** The single most far-reaching extension of the 1945 settlement beyond the League precedent operated not within the Trusteeship System itself but through Chapter XI's Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories, which extended the principle of international interest from the narrow category of formal trust territories to the much broader universe of all non-self-governing territories administered by member states. This extension — analyzed in detail in subchapter 4.5 — was the textual mechanism by which the British Empire, the French Union, the Belgian Congo, the Portuguese colonial empire, and the Dutch East Indies, none of which were trust territories, were brought within the normative scope of the UN's interest in dependent peoples.

## 4.4 The Trusteeship Council and the Politics of Supervision

The Trusteeship Council convened for its first session in 1947 and operated for nearly half a century, suspending operations in 1994 following the independence of the last trust territory (Palau, the final component of the Trust Territory of the Pacific Islands, achieved independence in 1994). The eleven territories that came under trusteeship between 1946 and 1950 were drawn principally from the residue of the League Mandates System, supplemented by the former Italian Somaliland (placed under Italian administration as a trust territory in 1950) and the Pacific island territories captured from Japan.

The conversion of former mandates into UN trust territories proceeded through individual trusteeship agreements negotiated between the United Nations and the proposed administering authority, with the agreements approved by the General Assembly (for ordinary trusts) or by the Security Council (for strategic trusts). The territories that completed this conversion and were placed under trusteeship included Tanganyika (British administration, formerly the British Class B mandate); Cameroon (in two parts: British Cameroons under British administration and French Cameroon under French administration, formerly the Class B mandates divided between the two powers); Togo (similarly in two parts: British Togoland and French Togoland); Ruanda-Urundi (Belgian administration, formerly the Belgian Class B mandate); Western Samoa (New Zealand administration, formerly the New Zealand Class C mandate); the Pacific Islands (United States administration of the former Japanese Class C mandates over the Marshall, Caroline, and Mariana Islands, designated as the strategic trust); New Guinea (Australian administration, formerly the Australian Class C mandate); and Nauru (Australian administration in trust on behalf of Australia, New Zealand, and the United Kingdom). Italian Somaliland was placed under Italian administration in 1950 as a trust territory with the explicit objective of independence within a ten-year period — a deliberate departure from the open-ended formulation of trusteeship objectives, marking the territory as the first trust with an explicit timetable.

The most consequential exception to this conversion was South West Africa. As Chapter 2 noted, South Africa declined to convert its Class C mandate over South West Africa into a UN trusteeship and instead sought, through the late 1940s, to incorporate the territory formally into the Union. The General Assembly rejected South Africa's incorporation proposal in December 1946 and called on South Africa to place the territory under trusteeship; South Africa's continued refusal initiated the long sequence of General Assembly resolutions, International Court of Justice advisory opinions, and contentious proceedings that culminated in the 1971 ICJ Advisory Opinion on Namibia and the eventual independence of Namibia in 1990. The case of South West Africa was juridically consequential for two reasons: it produced authoritative international determinations (notably the ICJ's 1950 Advisory Opinion) that the territory retained an international status that South Africa could not unilaterally dissolve; and it established the principle that the supervisory responsibilities of the international community over former mandates persisted regardless of an administering power's refusal to enter into a trusteeship agreement. The 1950 ICJ opinion drew explicitly on "the use of the words 'sacred trust of civilization' in Article 22 of the Covenant" to ground its conclusion that the territory's international status survived the dissolution of the League. The case thus inscribed the continuity between the Mandates and Trusteeship regimes into the jurisprudence of the post-1945 era while simultaneously demonstrating the limits of administering-power compliance with the new institutional architecture.

The substantive work of the Trusteeship Council across its active life can be characterized in terms of three principal functions. First, the receipt and examination of annual reports from administering authorities, which proceeded along lines that broadly resembled the PMC's annual examination of mandatory reports but operated within the more politicized and publicly visible context of the UN system. Second, the receipt and examination of petitions from inhabitants of trust territories — a function that, as noted in subchapter 4.3, was significantly strengthened compared to the PMC's procedure through the admission of direct petitions and the practice of oral hearings of petitioners. Third, the conduct of visiting missions to trust territories, a new procedural innovation that gave the Council independent informational access and allowed petitioners on-territory contact with the international supervisory body. The combination of these functions converted the Trusteeship Council into a working international forum in which administering-power conduct was subject to sustained, if politically constrained, scrutiny.

The political dynamics of the Council were shaped by the parity composition built into Chapter XIII. The administering-state bloc — comprising the United Kingdom, France, Belgium, Australia, New Zealand, the United States, and Italy — voted together with some regularity on questions touching the prerogatives of administering authorities, but it could be outvoted by the combined non-administering members when the latter mobilized around specific issues. The Council's reports to the General Assembly — particularly through the 1950s, as the General Assembly's anti-colonial majority consolidated — provided substantive material that the Assembly's Fourth Committee could deploy in its broader anti-colonial work. The relationship between the Trusteeship Council and the broader political dynamics of the General Assembly is the principal subject of Chapter 5; here it suffices to note that the Council's procedurally innovative supervisory work, while operating within significant structural constraints, fed into the broader anti-colonial transformation of the UN that characterized the late 1950s and 1960s.

The constraints on the Council's authority were nevertheless substantial and deserve emphasis. The Council, like the PMC before it, had no executive authority to compel administering-power compliance with its findings; its competence was limited to the formulation of recommendations to the General Assembly or the Security Council. The strategic-trust category placed the most strategically significant trust territory (the Pacific Islands under United States administration) under the supervisory authority of the Security Council, where the United States as a permanent member with veto power was effectively insulated from outvoting on substantive matters. The "conservatory clause" of Article 80, preserving existing rights and the terms of existing instruments until they were superseded by trusteeship agreements, allowed administering powers significant negotiating leverage in the design of the individual trusteeship agreements through which each territory's regime was specified. And the Trusteeship Council's jurisdiction was confined to trust territories themselves; the much broader universe of non-self-governing territories — to which the Council had no supervisory access — was addressed through the institutionally weaker mechanisms of Chapter XI, which the next subchapter examines.

## 4.5 Chapter XI and the Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories

The Declaration Regarding Non-Self-Governing Territories — Chapter XI of the Charter, comprising Articles 73 and 74 — emerged from the San Francisco negotiations as the most far-reaching textual extension of international interest to dependent territories that the conference was capable of producing, and as one of its most carefully calibrated compromises. Its provisions deserve close textual attention precisely because the institutional and political weight that they would eventually bear, examined in detail in Chapter 5, depended on the specific language in which they were framed.

The genesis of Chapter XI lay principally in proposals advanced at San Francisco by Australia, with significant supporting participation by New Zealand and amendments from the United States and the United Kingdom. The Australian delegation, led by H. V.
