The principal substantive output of the Conference was the ten-point Declaration on the Promotion of World Peace and Cooperation — known variously as the *Dasasila Bandung* (Bandung's Ten Principles), the Bandung Spirit, the Bandung Declaration, or the Ten Principles of Peaceful Coexistence — adopted unanimously as item G in the final communiqué.[^30] The Declaration incorporated "the principles of the United Nations Charter as well as Five Principles of Peaceful Coexistence" — the principles previously articulated in negotiations between India and China in 1954: mutual respect for territorial integrity and sovereignty, non-aggression, non-interference in internal affairs, equality and mutual benefit, and peaceful coexistence.[^30][^31] The Declaration "later became a foundational framework for the Non-Aligned Movement, influencing diplomatic relations among developing nations during the Cold War."[^30]

The substantive treatment of colonialism within the Declaration deserves close analytical attention. The political content of the Conference's anti-colonial commitment was articulated through the formula that "colonialism in all of its manifestations" was condemned — a formulation that, as the institutional record explicitly notes, "implicitly censur[ed] the Soviet Union, as well as the West."[^30] The formula emerged from "major debate" at the Conference "centered on the question of whether Soviet policies in Eastern Europe and Central Asia should be censured along with Western colonialism," with a memo submitted by 'The Moslem Nations under Soviet Imperialism' "accusing the Soviet authorities of massacres and mass deportations in Muslim regions, but it was never debated."[^30] The consensus formula thus accommodated both Western and Soviet anti-colonial criticism within a single rhetorical framework that did not require Conference participants to take substantive positions on the political character of Soviet rule in Eastern Europe or Central Asia — a calibrated ambiguity that would prove central to the subsequent Non-Aligned Movement's positioning vis-à-vis the two superpowers.

The substantive economic dimensions of the Bandung consensus, while less prominent than the anti-colonial commitments, anticipated themes that would acquire greater institutional weight in the subsequent G77 and NIEO trajectories. The final Communique "underscored the need for developing countries to loosen their economic dependence on the leading industrialised nations by providing technical assistance to one another through the exchange of experts and technical assistance for developmental projects, as well as the exchange of technological know-how and the establishment of regional training and research institutes."[^30] The substantive content of this economic dimension — the emphasis on South-South cooperation, the framing of dependence on industrialized nations as a substantive problem, the institutional infrastructure of technical assistance and research — supplied the discursive seeds of the subsequent NIEO project.

The relationship between Bandung and the subsequent Non-Aligned Movement was not one of direct continuity. As the institutional record clarifies, the Conference "was a step towards the eventual creation of the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) yet the two initiatives ran in parallel during the 1960s, even coming in confrontation with one another prior to the 2nd Cairo NAM Conference in 1964."[^30] The intervening period was characterized by the conjunction of two developments. The first was the convening of the Afro-Asian People's Solidarity Conference at Cairo in September 1957, which extended the institutional infrastructure of Afro-Asian solidarity beyond the state-to-state framework of Bandung.[^30] The second was the diplomatic work of Tito, Nehru, and Nasser through the late 1950s in constructing the institutional basis for what would become NAM — a project whose substantive distinction from the Bandung tradition operated principally through its universalist rather than regionalist conception of membership, as subchapter 8.2 will examine.

The First Conference of Heads of State or Government of Non-Aligned Countries was convened at Belgrade in September 1961 under the convening leadership of Yugoslav President Josip Broz Tito, with substantial participation by Nehru of India and Nasser of the United Arab Republic. The Belgrade Summit formally inaugurated the Non-Aligned Movement as an institutional vehicle distinct from the Bandung tradition; its substantive significance lay in the formalization of non-alignment as a substantive diplomatic commitment, the establishment of the institutional practice of periodic summit conferences, and the construction of a membership architecture that extended beyond the Asian-African geographic frame of Bandung to include European (Yugoslavia) and Latin American (Cuba) participation. The institutional record of the subsequent Second Summit at Cairo in October 1964 documents that at Belgrade, twenty-five states had participated; by Cairo, that number had expanded to fifty-seven, of which forty-seven were full members and ten were observers.[^32]

The ideological foundations of non-alignment, articulated through the substantive positions of the founding leaders, can be characterized along three principal dimensions. The first dimension was the rejection of bloc politics: as Nasser would articulate at the Cairo Summit three years later, "non-alignment is not the third bloc but instead opposition to bloc divisions, and is active rather than passive policy."[^32] The second dimension was the affirmation of sovereign autonomy: the substantive content of non-alignment was articulated principally through the assertion of the sovereign equality of states, the principle of non-interference in internal affairs, and the right of all states to determine their own development free from external coercion. The third dimension was the substantive engagement with the principal questions of international politics — colonialism, peace and security, disarmament, the structure of the international economic order — from the perspective of the post-colonial states' substantive concerns rather than the bloc orientations of the Cold War confrontation.

The continuities with the interwar anti-imperial internationalism examined in Chapter 3 are substantial. The League Against Imperialism founded at Brussels in February 1927, as Chapter 3 examined, had assembled a transnational anti-colonial coalition whose substantive content included the linkage of formal political independence to economic emancipation, the assertion of a self-organized anti-colonial international voice independent of the framing operations of the metropolitan international system, and the construction of personal and intellectual networks across regional anti-colonial currents. The Bandung and Belgrade traditions reproduced these features in transformed institutional context: the post-1955 movement operated from the position of independent states whose sovereignty was internationally recognized, its diplomatic capacities included the use of voting majorities in multilateral forums that the interwar coalition had not been able to approach, and its substantive engagement with both Cold War blocs operated from a position of declared autonomy rather than the tactical alignment with the Soviet-led current that the LAI's hybrid coalitional architecture had embodied. The personal continuities are likewise substantial: Nehru's trajectory from the Brussels Congress of 1927 to the Belgrade Summit of 1961 exemplifies the transmission of interwar anti-colonial internationalism into the post-war Third World project, even as the institutional and strategic logics of the post-war project differed substantially from the interwar conjuncture.

## 8.2 The Evolution of NAM through the 1960s and 1970s: Cairo, Lusaka, Algiers, and Beyond

The institutional development of the Non-Aligned Movement across the period from Belgrade 1961 to Havana 1979 operated through a succession of triennial heads-of-state conferences whose substantive content registered the progressive shift of the Movement's center of gravity from predominantly political concerns toward economic-justice agendas, and the corresponding tensions over membership criteria, ideological orientation, and the relationship to the broader institutional architecture of post-colonial diplomacy.

The Second Summit of Non-Aligned Countries was convened at Cairo from 5 to 10 October 1964 under the chairmanship of Egyptian President Gamal Abdel Nasser, who assumed the chairmanship of the Movement from Tito at the opening of the conference.[^32] The Cairo Summit assembled fifty-seven countries, of which forty-seven were full members and ten were observers; twenty-six countries were represented by their respective heads of state and ten by heads of government.[^32] The substantive content of the opening speeches articulated the principal concerns that would structure the Movement's subsequent trajectory. Nasser's opening remarks "commented on the shifting international context since the first summit in Belgrade in 1961," articulating the substantive content of non-alignment as "not the third bloc but instead opposition to bloc divisions," and calling for "the abolition of direct and hidden imperialism, action on socio-economic inequalities, and prevention of future obstructions by major powers to colonies negotiating their independence."[^32] President Sukarno of Indonesia "commented on peaceful coexistence among major powers, whose direct confrontation would lead to mutual destruction," and noted "the stagnant or even worsening security situation for developing countries, particularly in Southeast Asia, Middle East, Cyprus, Congo and Latin America."[^32] President of Ghana Kwame Nkrumah "identified four major causes of internal tensions: the division of Germany and Berlin; anticolonial liberation struggles; Cold War ideological divisions; and the superpower arms race."[^32] The Cairo Summit took place against the immediate backdrop of the August 1964 Gulf of Tonkin incident, which had triggered the Second Indochina War.[^33]

The single most consequential substantive question resolved at the Cairo Summit concerned membership criteria for the Movement. The contested question was whether the Movement should adopt a universalist or regionalist approach. "Yugoslavia advocated for a universalist approach, in which the movement would be open to all the non-aligned countries regardless of geography, notably in Europe and Latin America," while "Indonesia advocated for a narrower Afro-Asian regionalism," an approach "strongly supported by China, [which] was intended to present the movement as a continuation of the Bandung Conference."[^32] The contestation between these approaches was not merely procedural; it operated as the institutional expression of a deeper substantive divergence over the relationship between non-alignment as a substantive diplomatic commitment and Afro-Asian solidarity as a regionally framed political project.

The political resolution of this tension at Cairo operated through the simultaneous diplomatic processes that the two camps were conducting. As the institutional record documents, "the two approaches competed with an Indonesian-Chinese plan to organize the Second Bandung Conference in late 1963 or early 1964 and an Indian, Egyptian and Yugoslav plan for the second Non-Aligned conference."[^32] The parallel preparatory meetings — the Colombo preparatory meeting for the Second Non-Aligned Summit and the delayed Jakarta preparatory meeting for the Second Bandung Conference — registered the institutional bifurcation, and the substantive resolution emerged from the convergence of three factors. The first was the broader support for the Non-Aligned approach: the Second Bandung preparatory meeting was "ultimately supported by only Ghana, Iran, Cambodia, Guinea and Mali (Cambodia, Guinea and Mali supported both initiatives)."[^32] The second was the substantive intervention of Sri Lankan Prime Minister Sirimavo Bandaranaike, who "confronted those criticisms by stressing the indivisibility of world peace."[^32] The third was the subsequent collapse of the Second Bandung initiative under the convergent pressures of the Sino-Soviet split, the 1965 Algerian coup d'état, the ouster of Sukarno in Indonesia, the 1966 Ghanaian coup d'état, and Egypt's growing closeness to the Soviet Union.[^30][^32]

The substantive outcome of the Cairo resolution was decisive for the institutional trajectory of NAM. The universalist approach was consolidated as the operative framework of the Movement, with the Cairo Summit including substantial Latin American and European participation: Cuba was represented by President Osvaldo Dorticós Torrado as a full member, while Argentina, Bolivia, Brazil, Chile, Mexico, Uruguay, Venezuela, and Finland participated as observers.[^32] The institutional consequence was that NAM's subsequent development would proceed under a framework that accommodated the diverse geographic constituencies of the post-colonial project, rather than the narrower Afro-Asian regionalism that Indonesian and Chinese diplomacy had advocated. The substantive consequence for the broader Third World project was that the diplomatic infrastructure of NAM could be coordinated with the broader Group of 77 architecture examined in subchapter 8.3.

The Third Summit at Lusaka in September 1970 under the chairmanship of Zambian President Kenneth Kaunda registered the substantive shift toward institutionalization and reactivation of the Movement following the disruptions of the mid-1960s.[^34] Kaunda's opening of the conference "underlin[ed] non-alignment as 'the natural choice at the time of increased hostility created by ideological conflicts in the bipolar world.'"[^34] The Lusaka Summit's substantive contributions included the institutionalization of cooperation mechanisms among non-aligned countries and the reactivation of the Movement in international relations following the political turbulence of the mid-1960s. The Summit also marked the geographic shift of the Movement's center of gravity toward sub-Saharan Africa, registering the consolidation of post-colonial sovereignty across the continent.

The Fourth Summit at Algiers in September 1973 under the chairmanship of Algerian President Houari Boumédiène constituted the substantive turning point in the Movement's evolution toward the economic-justice agenda that the subsequent NIEO project would crystallize. Algeria had emerged from the eight-year war of independence (1954–1962) as one of the principal voices of radical anti-imperialism within the post-colonial coalition, and "under leaders like Houari Boumédiène, Algeria became a staunch advocate for the movement's objectives, particularly in its push for economic justice."[^35] The substantive content of the Algiers Summit registered this orientation: the Summit had been preceded by the convening at Algiers of the First Ministerial Meeting of the Group of 77, and "even before the fourth NAM summit in Algiers in 1973, Algiers hosted the First Ministerial... declaration and action programme for a New International Economic" Order.[^36] The institutional convergence at Algiers of the NAM political infrastructure with the G77 economic-diplomacy infrastructure supplied the coalitional architecture through which the subsequent NIEO declarations would be advanced at the General Assembly's Sixth Special Session in April–May 1974.

The substantive coordination achieved across an increasingly heterogeneous NAM membership across the 1970s deserves analytical emphasis. The Movement had expanded from the twenty-five participating states at Belgrade in 1961 to substantially larger membership by Algiers (1973), Colombo (1976), and Havana (1979). The expansion accommodated states with substantially divergent political institutions — parliamentary democracies, single-party socialist states, monarchies, military regimes — and with diverse Cold War orientations ranging from explicit non-alignment to varying degrees of accommodation with one or the other superpower. The substantive coordination operated through the convergence of these heterogeneous constituencies on a limited but politically central set of substantive commitments: anti-colonialism (and increasingly anti-apartheid), self-determination, sovereignty over natural resources, the substantive transformation of the international economic order, and the principle of non-alignment from the Cold War blocs.

The role of the principal individual leaders in shaping the Movement's trajectory deserves brief comment. Tito of Yugoslavia, as the founding chair of NAM at Belgrade and as a continuing diplomatic presence across the subsequent decades, supplied the consistent advocacy of the universalist approach to membership that the Cairo Summit had consolidated; Yugoslavia's distinctive position as a European socialist state outside the Warsaw Pact made its substantive non-alignment institutionally durable. Nasser of Egypt, until his death in September 1970 between the Lusaka and Algiers Summits, supplied the principal Arab voice within NAM. Nkrumah of Ghana, until his deposition in the February 1966 coup, supplied the principal voice of pan-African radicalism within the Movement; his substantive contributions to the conceptual framework of neocolonialism would condition the subsequent elaboration of dependency theory and the NIEO project. Boumédiène of Algeria supplied the principal voice of radical economic-justice politics within NAM across the 1970s, and his chairmanship of the Algiers Summit of 1973 operationalized the substantive turn toward the NIEO project. Fidel Castro of Cuba, whose chairmanship of NAM from the Havana Summit of 1979 onward was institutionally consequential, supplied a substantive alignment with the Soviet Union that generated internal tensions within the Movement over the substantive content of non-alignment.

The internal tensions over universalist versus regionalist approaches, the Sino-Soviet split's effects on the Movement, and the progressive shift from political to economic concerns thus structured the institutional trajectory of NAM across the 1960s and 1970s. The Movement's substantive coordination across this heterogeneity operated through the convergence of its constituencies on the limited set of central commitments that the post-1960 anti-colonial project had codified, and through the institutional infrastructure of triennial summit conferences whose substantive content registered the cumulative consolidation of the project. The transition to the substantive economic-justice agenda of the NIEO, which Algiers 1973 inaugurated, operated through the institutional convergence with the Group of 77 architecture that subchapter 8.3 now examines.

## 8.3 The Group of 77 and UNCTAD: The Institutionalization of Southern Economic Diplomacy

The institutional architecture through which the substantive economic-justice agenda of the Third World project was operationalized within the UN system was the Group of 77 (G77), founded at the conclusion of the first session of the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD I) at Geneva in 1964 and elaborated across the subsequent decades into the principal coalition of developing countries within the broader UN system.

The founding of UNCTAD itself, examined in Chapter 7, is essential to the G77's institutional architecture. UNCTAD was established by General Assembly Resolution 1995 (XIX) of 30 December 1964 as an organ of the General Assembly, following the first session of the Conference held in Geneva from 23 March to 16 June 1964 and attended by 120 states.[^37] The institutional drivers of UNCTAD's creation, as the authoritative institutional record summarizes, operated along four convergent dimensions: the economic dimension of deteriorating developing-country positions in world trade despite the dynamism of the post-1950 world economy; the political dimension of decolonization having generated new post-colonial states that "felt that the world economic system was systematically biased against their development goals, and put forward the need for reform to make it more democratic and development-friendly"; the analytical dimension of the structuralist economics developed at ECLA under Prebisch, which had "developed the theory of the secular deterioration of the terms of trade of developing countries"; and the legal-institutional dimension of the failure of the Havana Charter and the limited character of the GATT framework.[^37]

The diplomatic process that produced UNCTAD operated through several principal moments. Early efforts by developing countries for the calling of an international conference on international trade problems had been advanced in 1961, with General Assembly Resolution 1710 (XVI) of December 1961 requesting the UN Secretary-General to consult Member States on the advisability of convening such a conference.[^37] A decisive political impulse was given by the Conference on the Problems of Economic Development that took place in Cairo in July 1962, attended by 36 African, Asian, and Latin American countries; the conference issued a declaration that "strongly recommended the early convening of an international conference on trade and development under the aegis of the United Nations (UN) to consider 'all vital questions relating to international trade, primary commodity trade and economic relations between developing and developed countries.'"[^37] The recommendation was accepted by UNGA Resolution 1785 (XVII) of 8 December 1962, and the conference took place at Geneva from 23 March to 15 June 1964 with Raúl Prebisch appointed as Secretary-General.[^37]

The substantive content of UNCTAD I produced two institutional outputs of fundamental importance for the subsequent trajectory of Southern economic diplomacy. The first was the conference's substantive recommendations: "15 general principles and 13 special principles to govern international trade relations and trade policies conducive to development," together with "a large number of other specific recommendations" addressing commodities, manufactured exports of developing countries, resources for development and debt adjustments, and increased exchanges with countries of Eastern Europe.[^37] The voting record on these recommendations registered the substantive division between developing and industrialized states: "The prime supporters of the principles and recommendations were the developing countries, with many developed countries voting against or abstaining. In particular, the United States voted against 15 of the 28 principles, and abstained with respect to seven others."[^37] The pattern of these votes registered, in the substantive terrain of international trade, the same North–South cleavage that Chapter 7 had identified in the broader General Assembly voting record.

The second institutional output was the establishment of the Group of 77 itself. As the authoritative institutional record summarizes, the conference produced "two important institutional results. The first was the recommendation, later accepted by the UNGA, to establish UNCTAD itself as a permanent institution; the second was the creation of the Group of 77 (G77), composed of all developing countries that attended the conference, which has become a de facto main actor in the decision-making processes of UNCTAD and the UN more generally."[^37]

The mandate of UNCTAD, codified in General Assembly Resolution 1995 (XIX), articulated five principal aims: "(a) To promote international trade, especially with a view to accelerating economic development...; (b) To formulate principles and policies on international trade and related problems of economic development; (c) To make proposals for putting the said principles and policies into effect...; (d)...to review and facilitate the coordination of activities of other institutions within the United Nations system in the field of international trade...; (e) To initiate action for the negotiation and adoption of multilateral legal instruments in the field of trade...; (f) To be available as a centre for harmonizing the trade and related development policies of Governments and regional economic groupings."[^37] The institutional architecture established by the resolution included the conference itself as the highest organ, meeting normally at intervals of no more than four years; the Trade and Development Board as the highest permanent organ; and a permanent secretariat based in Geneva headed by the Secretary-General of the conference.[^37]

The institutional architecture of the G77 across the chapters in Geneva, New York, Nairobi, Paris, Rome, Vienna, and Washington (the latter being known as the Group of 24) was elaborated across the subsequent decades. As the institutional record of the G77's fortieth anniversary documents, "the prospects of achieving the objectives of the Group are closely related to our capacity to maintain and improve coordination of the activities that we undertake in the different Chapters of the Group of 77 within the United Nations system located in Geneva, Nairobi, Paris, Rome, Vienna and Washington, D.C. (Group of 24)."[^38] The substantive significance of this multi-chapter architecture was that the G77 operated not as a single coordinating body but as a distributed coalition with chapters within each of the principal venues of the UN system, enabling the substantive coordination of Southern positions across the diverse institutional terrain that the post-1945 UN system had inherited.

The group system of negotiation through which UNCTAD operated deserves particular analytical attention. As the institutional record summarizes, "the decision-making process in UNCTAD is based on what is known as the group system, whereby member countries are divided into groups which negotiate on behalf of their members with the other groups."[^37] The legal bases for the system, contained in Resolution 1995 (XIX), "established four lists of States: List A comprising African and Asian countries; List B, developed market economy countries; List C, Latin American and Caribbean countries; and List D, socialist countries."[^37] The political basis for the group system as a substantive negotiating mechanism "was provided by developing countries organizing themselves as a group (the G77), at the first session of UNCTAD. From then on and until the eighth session of UNCTAD in 1992 the negotiation of resolutions took place essentially between the G77 and Group B, with Group D coming in in such areas as were of particular interest to its members."[^37] The G77 has 134 members, including China, and "is composed of three regional groups, namely Africa, Asia, and Latin America and the Caribbean," with each regional group agreeing internally on a negotiating position and taking it to the group as a whole before negotiation with other groups.[^37]

The substantive work of UNCTAD across its first 25 years, as the authoritative institutional record systematizes, addressed eight principal areas. **Commodities** work focused initially "on price stabilization arrangements and compensatory financing," with main efforts including "the adoption of the Integrated Programme for Commodities" at UNCTAD IV in Nairobi in 1976 "and the related establishment of the Common Fund for Commodities (CFC) in 1980 to finance commodity price stabilization and commodity development."[^37][^39][^40] **Trade in manufactures** work focused on "facilitating access to developing country exports of manufactures and semi-manufactures in developed country markets," with the thrust "directed to achieving international acceptance of the principle of non-reciprocity in trade between developed and developing countries and the setting up of a generalized system of trade preferences for the latter."[^37] **Money and finance** work emphasized "issues of international liquidity, resource flows to developing countries, international assistance and external debt, and the structure of decision-making in the Bretton Woods institution," with an UNCTAD resolution in 1978 on debt relief to poorer developing countries leading "18 developed country donors [to adopt] measures in this respect."[^37] **Global interdependence** work involved UNCTAD in "the two main attempts in the 1970s to restructure the global economic system" — the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States adopted by UNGA Resolution 3281 (XXIX) of December 1974, and the NIEO declarations of UNGA Resolutions 3201 (S-VI) and 3202 (S-VI) of May 1974.[^37] **Technology transfer** work involved the negotiation of "an international code of conduct on technology transfer... in UNCTAD between 1972 and 1985, but no agreement was reached and the effort was abandoned."[^37] **Shipping and insurance** work concentrated on helping developing countries increase their share in seaboard cargo, with the negotiation of a code of conduct for liner conferences in 1973 and its adoption in April 1974.[^37] **Economic cooperation among developing countries** work focused on the establishment of a mechanism for trade preferences among developing countries, leading to the conclusion of the Agreement on the Global System of Trade Preferences among Developing Countries in April 1988.[^37] Work on **least developed, landlocked, and island developing countries** designated UNCTAD as "the focal point within the UN system for work on the problems of the least developed countries."[^37]

The institutional significance of Raúl Prebisch's role as the founding Secretary-General of UNCTAD from 1964 to 1969 cannot be overstated. As the authoritative institutional history of UNCTAD's birth summarizes, "in the 60s with the establishment of ITO stalled the UN system was missing an organization or body analyzing trade and development from not least the developing countries perspective. It was demanded by the newly formed G 77. In 1964 these demands were met at the first UNCTAD Conference in Geneva; It's first Secretary General - Raul Prebisch came from a continent that was teeming with revolutionary spirit to change the present world order. He gave UNCTAD a head start and became the intellectual leader of the New International Economic Order that came to dominate the UN debates in the late 60s & early 70s."[^41] Prebisch's substantive analytical contribution, examined in detail in subchapter 8.5, supplied the conceptual infrastructure on which both UNCTAD's institutional work and the subsequent NIEO project would build.

## 8.4 NAM and G77 Compared: Political Non-Alignment versus Economic Coordination

The institutional architecture of post-colonial diplomacy across the 1960s and 1970s operated through the parallel and complementary work of two principal vehicles whose distinct ideological emphases, organizational architectures, and operational functions structured the broader Third World project. The Non-Aligned Movement and the Group of 77 were not duplicative institutions; they performed substantively distinct functions whose coordinated operation supplied the post-colonial states with a diplomatic infrastructure of substantial coherence.

The principal axis of differentiation between NAM and G77 operated along the political–economic dimension of substantive emphasis. NAM's principal substantive commitment was articulated through the principle of non-alignment from the Cold War blocs — what Nasser at Cairo 1964 characterized as "opposition to bloc divisions."[^32] The substantive content of NAM's work centered on the questions of peace and security, decolonization, anti-imperialism, sovereign autonomy, and the broader political relations among states in the international system. G77's principal substantive commitment, by contrast, was articulated through the economic-justice agenda — the substantive transformation of the international economic order, the renegotiation of the terms of international trade, the regulation of transnational corporations, the reform of international finance, and the substantive content of permanent sovereignty over natural resources. The substantive content of G77's work centered on the questions of commodities, manufactures, finance, technology, and the broader economic relations between developing and industrialized states.

The organizational architectures of the two vehicles registered this functional differentiation. NAM operated as a summit-based political movement with triennial heads-of-state conferences as its principal institutional moments, supplemented by ministerial meetings and the coordinating work of the chair country between summits. The institutional infrastructure of NAM was deliberately light: there was no permanent secretariat of substantial scale, no fixed institutional location, and no formal binding decision-making procedures comparable to those of the UN system. The substantive significance of this light institutional architecture was that NAM operated as a coordinating diplomatic vehicle through which the substantive positions of the post-colonial states on questions of high politics could be aligned and publicly articulated, rather than as a permanent decision-making body with substantive policy outputs of its own. G77, by contrast, operated as a permanent UN-based negotiating coalition with chapters within each of the principal venues of the UN system (Geneva, New York, Nairobi, Paris, Rome, Vienna, and Washington), with substantive coordination practices that translated the demographic majorities of the post-1960 General Assembly into operational diplomatic positions, and with institutional continuity grounded in the permanent character of UNCTAD and the broader UN architecture within which the G77 chapters operated.[^38]

The membership overlaps and divergences between NAM and G77 deserve analytical emphasis. The two vehicles operated with substantially overlapping but not identical memberships. NAM's membership, anchored in the principle of non-alignment from the Cold War blocs, was somewhat more restrictive in formal terms — though in practice, the Movement accommodated a wide range of orientations across its constituency, as Cuba's substantive alignment with the Soviet Union under Castro's chairmanship from 1979 illustrated. G77's membership, anchored in the substantive criteria of developing-country status within the UN economic architecture, included the substantial majority of developing countries irrespective of their Cold War positioning; the Group has 134 members, including China.[^37] The membership overlaps were substantial — most NAM members were also G77 members, and most G77 members were also NAM members — but the formal membership criteria operated through distinct substantive bases that conditioned the diplomatic identity of each vehicle.

The complementary functions of the two vehicles operated through a substantive division of labor that emerged across the 1960s and 1970s. The Algiers NAM Summit of September 1973 supplied the political articulation of the substantive demands that the subsequent G77-led negotiations at the General Assembly Sixth Special Session of April–May 1974 would crystallize into the NIEO declarations.[^35][^36] The institutional convergence at Algiers — with the First Ministerial Meeting of the G77 having been hosted in Algiers prior to the NAM Summit, generating the "declaration and action programme for a New International Economic" Order — registered the substantive coordination between NAM political mobilization and G77 economic-diplomatic execution.[^36] The pattern of this coordination was institutionally durable across the period: NAM Summits supplied the political momentum and the public articulation of substantive demands, while G77 chapters within UNCTAD and the broader UN system supplied the technical and diplomatic infrastructure through which those demands were translated into negotiated resolutions, declarations, and codes of conduct.

The tensions and synergies between the respective agendas of NAM and G77 operated principally through the question of the relationship between political and economic content. The substantive ambition of the NIEO project, examined in subsequent subchapters, integrated the political principles of self-determination, sovereignty, and non-alignment with the substantive economic demands for restructuring the international economic order; the institutional coordination of NAM and G77 across the 1970s reflected the substantive coherence of this integrated project. The tensions arose principally where the political orientations of particular NAM members generated divergences from the substantive economic-justice agenda of G77 — for example, where the strategic alignment of particular states with one or the other superpower conditioned their substantive positions on the demands for restructuring international economic relations. But these tensions operated as friction within a substantively coherent coalition rather than as fundamental fractures in the project's institutional architecture, and the durability of the parallel operation of NAM and G77 across the subsequent decades registers the substantive coherence of the institutional logic that the 1960s and 1970s had constructed.

The consequences of the parallel operation of the two vehicles for the coherence and effectiveness of Southern diplomacy were substantial. The institutional architecture supplied the post-colonial states with two distinct but coordinated channels through which their substantive positions could be advanced: the high-political channel of NAM Summits, with their public mobilization and articulation of substantive demands, and the technical-diplomatic channel of G77 chapters within the UN system, with their continuous negotiating work and their permanent institutional infrastructure. The substantive coherence of the broader Third World project across the 1970s operated through this dual architecture. The substantive defeats of the NIEO project across the 1980s, examined in subchapter 8.9, operated through the convergent failure of both channels under the pressures of the debt crisis and the broader transformation of the international political economy.

## 8.5 Intellectual Origins of the NIEO: Structuralist Economics, Dependency Theory, and the Prebisch Legacy

The substantive content of the New International Economic Order project was conditioned by an intellectual genealogy whose principal lineage operated through the structuralist economics developed at the UN Economic Commission for Latin America (ECLA, also known by its Spanish acronym CEPAL) under the leadership of Raúl Prebisch from 1950 onward.

Raúl Prebisch's biographical and intellectual trajectory supplies the personal continuity through which the structuralist analytical framework was elaborated and institutionalized. Born in Tucumán, Argentina, in 1901, Prebisch studied at the University of Buenos Aires Faculty of Economic Sciences and held a series of positions in the Argentine public sector through the 1930s and 1940s, including Deputy Director of the Argentine Department of Statistics (1925–1927), Director of Economic Research for the National Bank of Argentina (1927–1930), Under-Secretary of Finance (1930–1932), and first Director-General of the Argentine Central Bank (1935–1948).[^42][^43] His intellectual trajectory across this period registered a substantive transformation: as a young man, his writing was "marked by a complete adherence to the idea of free-trade," but "in the 1930s, as a result of the Great Depression he 'converted' to protectionism."[^42] The substantive content of this conversion operated through Prebisch's recognition that "during the Great Depression the prices of primary products, such as agricultural goods, fell much more than the prices of manufactured secondary products" — an empirical observation whose theoretical elaboration would supply the foundation of the structuralist framework.[^42]

The institutional consolidation of Prebisch's analytical framework operated through his appointment as Executive Secretary of ECLA in 1950 and the publication of his foundational study *El desarrollo económico de la América Latina y algunos de sus principales problemas* (The Economic Development of Latin America and Its Principal Problems) in the same year.[^42][^44] Prebisch's central conceptual innovation was the division of the world economy into the "center," consisting of industrialized nations, and the "periphery," consisting of primary producers — a framework that "separated out the purely theoretical aspects of economics from the actual practice of trade and the power structures that underlie trading institutions and agreements."[^42] The substantive analytical content of the framework was articulated through the Prebisch–Singer hypothesis, developed contemporaneously and independently by Prebisch and the German economist Hans Singer: "the hypothesis begins with the observation that in the present world system the periphery produces primary goods to export to the center, and the centre produces secondary goods for export to the periphery. According to the hypothesis, as technology improves, the centre is able to retain the savings made, since it can retain higher wages and profits through developed unions and commercial institutions. At the periphery, companies and workers are weaker, and have to pass on technical savings to their customers in the form of lower prices. Prebisch pointed to the decline in the terms of trade between industrialised and non-industrialised countries, which meant peripheral nations had to export more to get the same value of industrial imports. Through this system, all of the benefits of technology and international trade would accrue to the centre."[^42]

The substantive policy implications of the structuralist framework, as Prebisch elaborated them across the 1950s and 1960s, can be characterized along six principal dimensions, systematized in the authoritative biographical treatment of his work.[^44] The first was the analysis of **structural inequality**: "the international economic system perpetuates structural inequality between developed and developing countries... the unequal exchange between the producers and exporters of primary commodities and the producers and exporters of manufactured goods leads to a deterioration of the terms of trade and a disparity in demand elasticity at the expense of emerging market economies."[^44] The second was the analysis of **dependency and underdevelopment**: this unequal exchange "has created a cycle of dependency and underdevelopment for developing countries: they become dependent on primary goods exports and vulnerable to commodity price fluctuations, which hinders industrialization and economic diversification."[^44] The third was the advocacy of **import substitution industrialization (ISI)** as a strategy to overcome dependency, "involv[ing] promoting domestic industries and reducing import dependence through protective measures such as tariffs and trade barriers."[^44] The fourth was the advocacy of **government intervention** through "countercyclical measures such as fiscal and monetary measures to manage aggregate demand and stabilize the economy during recessions," with industrialization also requiring "massive investment in infrastructure to accelerate growth and planning."[^44] The fifth was the concern with **income distribution and social welfare**, with Prebisch realizing "in the early 1960s that a decade of rapid growth had not benefited the majority of the low-income population, while a small percentage of the social structure became increasingly wealthy."[^44] The sixth was the advocacy of **international cooperation among developing countries**: Prebisch "increasingly worked towards greater international cooperation among them to participate in a North-South dialogue with more leverage. His work at UNCTAD crowned years of efforts in that sense."[^44]

The substantive nuance of Prebisch's relationship to ISI deserves brief comment. While "many scholars perceive Prebisch as supporting import substitution industrialization (ISI)... Prebisch criticized protectionism, especially that practiced by Juan Perón in Argentina, since 1956 and ISI since at least 1963."[^42] The substantive content of Prebisch's mature position thus operated through a more nuanced framework than the standard caricature of ISI advocacy would suggest: "he advocated industrialization and economic cooperation, including through trade, among developing countries," and at UNCTAD "he publicly condemned ISI as having failed to bring proper development."[^42] The substantive implication of this nuance is that the structuralist analytical framework on which the NIEO project would build was not reducible to the ISI policy prescription with which it was sometimes identified.

The relationship between Prebisch's analytical framework and the subsequent elaboration of dependency theory by Latin American and other Third World economists deserves brief analytical attention. As the authoritative summary of this trajectory documents, "during the 1960s, economists at the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLA) developed an extension of Prebisch's thoughts on structuralism into dependency theory, in which economic development of the periphery is seen as a nearly impossible task. While dependency theory is often seen to be opposed to the ECLA's original purpose, Prebisch's original framework was reformist, focused on improving developing nations' terms of trade through industrialization and regional integration. Dependency theorists drew directly on his center-periphery model. Prebisch's own thinking changed over time; by the 1970s, particularly through his work at UNCTAD, where he was increasingly critical of the international economic order about dependency concerns."[^42] The substantive content of dependency theory extended the structuralist framework into a more comprehensive analysis of the structural impossibility of peripheral development within the existing international economic system, supplying the analytical resources for the more radical demands of the subsequent NIEO project.

The institutional translation of structuralist and dependency analytical frameworks into the policy positions of UNCTAD operated through Prebisch's leadership of the organization from 1964 to 1969. His substantive policy contributions across the UNCTAD years included the development of the analytical framework on which UNCTAD's substantive work was based — *Towards a New Trade Policy for Development* (1964) and *Towards a New Global Strategy for Development* (1968), reports prepared by the Secretary-General — and the institutional advocacy through which the principles of non-reciprocity, preferential access to developed-country markets, and South-South cooperation were elaborated into the operative diplomatic positions of the developing countries.[^44] His "approach to development took a more trade-focused approach, advocating preferential access to the markets of developed countries and regional integration, building up trade between peripheral countries. Increasingly he stressed the extent to which developing countries had to bring growth by internal reforms rather than through external help."[^42]

Prebisch's eventual resignation from UNCTAD in 1969 registered the early signal of the institutional limits that the subsequent NIEO project would encounter at greater scale. As the institutional record documents, "Prebisch found his years at UNCTAD frustrating and 'sterile' as it became increasingly bureaucratic and failed to achieve its main objectives. His sudden resignation in 1969 signified his loss of patience with the organisation's failures."[^42] The substantive content of this frustration was Prebisch's recognition that the institutional capacities of UNCTAD were not sufficient to overcome the structural resistance of the principal industrialized states to the substantive transformations that the structuralist framework had identified as necessary. Prebisch's resignation thus prefigured the broader pattern that subchapter 8.9 will examine.

The longer-term intellectual trajectory of Prebisch's work registered a progressive radicalization of analytical critique. As his biographer documents, in his later work "his economic thinking increasingly evolved into historical thinking about capitalism, the historicity of ideas, and the future of the global economy."[^44] When Prebisch reflected on his long career, "he considered that experience taught him that the ideal solution to fix the world economic system should be 'a synthesis of both socialism and genuine economic liberalism'... the only path, in his view, to meet the 'tremendous historical responsibility' towards the future world."[^44] The substantive content of this synthetic position registers the persistent tension within the structuralist framework between its reformist origins and its progressively more critical analysis of the international economic order.

## 8.6 The 1974 Declarations: Formal Articulation of the New International Economic Order

The formal articulation of the New International Economic Order operated through three principal General Assembly resolutions adopted in 1974: Resolution 3201 (S-VI) of 1 May 1974, the Declaration on the Establishment of a New International Economic Order; Resolution 3202 (S-VI) of the same date, the Programme of Action on the Establishment of a New International Economic Order; and Resolution 3281 (XXIX) of 12 December 1974, the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States.[^45][^46][^47][^37]

The diplomatic context within which the 1974 declarations were articulated operated through the convergence of three principal dynamics. The first was the cumulative institutional momentum of the post-1960 Third World project: the consolidation of NAM through its successive summits, the institutional elaboration of the G77 across the chapters of the UN system, the substantive work of UNCTAD across the first three sessions (Geneva 1964, New Delhi 1968, Santiago 1972), and the analytical elaboration of the structuralist and dependency frameworks.[^37] The second was the substantive articulation of the NIEO project at the Algiers NAM Summit of September 1973, which had been preceded by the First Ministerial Meeting of the G77 at Algiers.[^35][^36] The third was the immediate shock of the 1973 oil crisis, in which the Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) imposed an oil embargo on countries supporting Israel during the October 1973 Yom Kippur War and subsequently raised the price of crude oil substantially. The substantive significance of the oil crisis for the NIEO project operated principally through the demonstration that coordinated action by a coalition of developing-country resource exporters could substantially affect the terms of international economic relations, supplying both an empirical model for the substantive demands of the NIEO and a substantial political resource through the windfall revenues accruing to OPEC member states. The convergence of these three dynamics generated the political conjuncture in which the General Assembly's Sixth Special Session — convened in April–May 1974 — produced the foundational declarations of the NIEO project.

The substantive content of Resolution 3201 (S-VI) — the Declaration on the Establishment of a New International Economic Order — articulated the principal commitments of the project: sovereign equality of states, self-determination of all peoples, full sovereignty over natural resources and economic activities, full participation of developing countries on the basis of equality in the solution of world economic problems, the right of every country to adopt the economic and social system it deemed appropriate, regulation and supervision of the activities of transnational corporations, a just and equitable relationship between the prices of raw materials and other primary commodities exported by developing countries and the prices of manufactures imported by them, preferential and non-reciprocal treatment for developing countries, transfer of technology to developing countries on favorable terms, and the broader restructuring of international economic relations along the principles articulated in the Declaration.

The substantive content of Resolution 3202 (S-VI) — the Programme of Action — operationalized the principles of the Declaration into specific commitments across the principal substantive domains of international economic relations. As the secondary literature on the NIEO documents, the principal substantive proposals included "improving trade terms for LDCs, establishing mechanisms for technology transfer, regulating multinational corporations, and reforming the international monetary system."[^48] The substantive content of these proposals operated across the four interlocking domains that subchapter 8.7 will examine.

The substantive content of Resolution 3281 (XXIX) — the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States, adopted by the General Assembly on 12 December 1974 — articulated the formal codification of the substantive principles of the NIEO project into a comprehensive normative instrument.[^46][^47] The Charter's substantive content extended the principles of the May 1974 declarations into a more elaborate codification, addressing the rights and duties of states across the principal domains of international economic relations including sovereignty over natural resources, the regulation of foreign investment, the conduct of transnational corporations, international trade, monetary and financial relations, and the broader framework of cooperation among states for economic development.

The institutional record of UNCTAD's role in the Charter's negotiation deserves brief comment. As the authoritative institutional history of UNCTAD summarizes, "the third session of UNCTAD in 1972 established a governmental working group to draft a Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States, the text of which was subsequently adopted by the UNGA through Resolution 3281 (XXIX) of 12 December 1974."[^37] The institutional vehicle through which the Charter was elaborated was thus the UNCTAD working group established at the 1972 Santiago session, whose substantive work translated the political mobilization of the Algiers NAM Summit and the immediate political conjuncture of 1973–1974 into the detailed textual provisions of the eventual codification.

The voting patterns on these declarations registered the substantive limits of the political consensus underlying the NIEO project. The Sixth Special Session declarations of 1 May 1974 (Resolutions 3201 and 3202) were adopted without a vote, registering the formal procedural consensus of the General Assembly — but the consensus was substantially qualified by the reservations and interpretative statements articulated by the principal industrialized states, whose substantive positions on the operative content of the declarations diverged significantly from the textual settlement. The Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States, adopted by recorded vote on 12 December 1974, registered the substantive limits of consensus more directly: the resolution was adopted by a supermajority but with negative votes registered by several of the principal industrialized states most directly engaged by the Charter's substantive provisions. The pattern of the Charter vote registered the substantive North–South cleavage that Chapter 7 had identified in the broader voting record, with the principal industrialized states substantively contesting the operative content of the codification even as the supermajority of the General Assembly affirmed the substantive principles of the NIEO project.

The interpretive significance of the gap between rhetorical breadth and substantive resistance supplies the analytical key to the subsequent trajectory of the NIEO project. The substantive normative codification accomplished at the General Assembly was substantial: the principles of the NIEO entered the formal record of international law as the articulated position of the supermajority of the Assembly, and the substantive content of those principles would condition the subsequent vocabulary of international economic relations across the decades. But the substantive resistance of the principal industrialized states to the operative content of the codification meant that the institutional vehicles through which the NIEO demands would have to be operationalized — the IMF, the World Bank, the GATT, the broader architecture of international economic governance — remained outside the demographic transformation of the UN proper and continued to operate under the weighted-governance arrangements that conditioned their substantive responsiveness to Southern demands. The gap between General Assembly codification and operative implementation was the structural feature of the post-1974 period that conditioned the eventual reversal of the NIEO agenda.

## 8.7 Substantive Demands: Commodities, Technology, Resource Sovereignty, and Financial Reform

The substantive content of the NIEO project can be systematically reconstructed across four interlocking domains whose substantive content engaged the principal categories of international economic relations identified by the structuralist analytical framework.

The first domain, **commodities**, occupied a central position in the substantive content of the NIEO project. The substantive rationale operated through the structuralist analysis of the secular deterioration of terms of trade for primary commodities. The substantive proposal advanced through the NIEO project was the Integrated Programme for Commodities (IPC), adopted at the fourth session of UNCTAD held at Nairobi in 1976.[^39][^40] As the institutional record of UNCTAD IV documents, "an integrated programme for commodities was adopted" at the conference, "adopted unanimously at the concluding session of UNCTAD IV on May 31, 1976."[^39] The IPC was "adopted at the 145th plenary meeting, 30 May 1976."[^40]

The substantive content of the Integrated Programme for Commodities encompassed several interlocking objectives: stabilization of international commodity prices through international commodity agreements covering a defined list of principal commodities; the establishment of a Common Fund for Commodities to finance the buffer stocks of the commodity agreements and to support commodity development; the expansion of processing of commodities in developing countries; the diversification of developing-country economies away from excessive dependence on primary commodity exports; and the broader integration of commodity policy with the substantive development objectives of the developing countries. The institutional translation of the Programme operated through the establishment of the Common Fund for Commodities, agreed in 1980. As the institutional summary of UNCTAD's commodities work documents, the principal commodities outputs across the period included "the adoption of the Integrated Programme for Commodities at the fourth session of UNCTAD... and the related establishment of the Common Fund for Commodities (CFC) in 1980 to finance commodity price stabilization and commodity development."[^37] The substantive content of this output fell substantially short of the ambitious objectives initially articulated.

The second domain, **technology transfer**, addressed the substantive question of access by developing countries to the technologies developed in the industrialized countries on terms compatible with developmental objectives. The substantive rationale operated through the structuralist analysis of technological dependency — the concentration of research, development, and intellectual property in the industrialized countries and the consequent dependence of developing countries on imported technology at terms substantially conditioned by the bargaining position of the technology-supplying firms, often transnational corporations. The substantive proposal advanced through the NIEO project was the negotiation of an International Code of Conduct on the Transfer of Technology, conducted within UNCTAD across the period from 1972 to 1985.[^37] As the institutional record documents, "an international code of conduct on technology transfer was negotiated in UNCTAD between 1972 and 1985, but no agreement was reached and the effort was abandoned."[^37] The failure of the technology code negotiations constituted one of the principal substantive defeats of the NIEO project.

The parallel negotiations on a Code of Conduct on Restrictive Business Practices, addressed to the regulation of anti-competitive practices by transnational corporations, achieved a more limited institutional output. As the institutional record summarizes, "UNCTAD was also the lead agency in the effort at introducing a global regime for competition in the late 1970s, which led to the adoption by the UNGA in December 1980 (UNGA Res 35/63 [5 December 1980]) of the Set of Multilaterally Agreed Equitable Principles and Rules for the Control of Restrictive Business Practices, a non-binding code on the prevention of abuses of positions of dominant market power."[^37] The non-binding character of the Set registered the substantive limits of the institutional consensus available for the regulation of transnational corporate conduct; the parallel negotiations toward a UN Code of Conduct on Transnational Corporations, conducted within the UN Centre on Transnational Corporations across the late 1970s and 1980s, similarly failed to produce a binding code, with the institutional record summarizing the trajectory of the "effort to reach agreement on the United Nations Code of Conduct on Transnational Corporations in the late 1970s and 1980s."[^49]

The third domain, **permanent sovereignty over natural resources**, articulated the substantive content of the political principle of sovereignty as applied to the economic resources within the territory of the sovereign state. The substantive antecedent of the NIEO articulation, examined in Chapter 7, was Resolution 1803 (XVII) of 14 December 1962, which had codified the principle of permanent sovereignty over natural resources in the context of the post-1960 transformation of the General Assembly. The NIEO project elaborated this principle through the 1974 declarations, with the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States articulating in particular detail the substantive content of permanent sovereignty including the right of states to nationalize foreign-owned property subject to appropriate compensation, the right to regulate foreign investment within their territory, and the right to coordinate production and pricing of natural resources through producer associations on the OPEC model. The substantive significance of the principle was operationalized through the wave of nationalizations of foreign-owned mineral and hydrocarbon enterprises that the principal resource-exporting developing countries conducted across the 1970s, and through the elaboration of the principle in the subsequent jurisprudence of international economic law.[^46]

The fourth domain, **financial and monetary reform**, addressed the substantive content of the international monetary and financial system within which developing-country external relations were conducted. The substantive content of the NIEO demands in this domain included: reform of the international monetary system, including the substantive role of the IMF and the structure of weighted voting within it; the allocation of Special Drawing Rights (SDRs) on terms that would benefit developing countries, including the proposal for an "SDR-aid link" through which a portion of new SDR allocations would be directed to developing countries; debt relief for the principal categories of developing-country debt accumulated across the post-independence period; and the expansion of development assistance from the industrialized countries to meet the target of 0.7 percent of donor GNI that the UN had identified as the appropriate scale of official development assistance.[^48]

The institutional output across the financial-reform domain was substantively mixed across the late 1970s. UNCTAD's work on debt relief operated through Trade and Development Board Resolution 165 S-IX of 11 March 1978 on "Debt and Development Problems of Developing Countries," which led to 18 developed-country donors adopting "measures in this respect."[^37] The institutional record characterizes this output as a non-binding resolution that nevertheless exerted substantive influence on donor practice, though the scale of the debt relief accomplished through this channel was modest in comparison to the substantive scale of developing-country debt that the subsequent 1980s crisis would expose. The 0.7 percent ODA target, which UNCTAD had been "instrumental in the adoption by the UNGA of," was not met by the principal industrialized states across the period; as the secondary literature on ODA performance documents, the United States' "ODA contribution was 0.18% of the U.S. GNI, which falls significantly short of the 0.7% target set by the United Nations."[^50] The Generalized System of Preferences (GSP), negotiated in UNCTAD and subsequently approved by GATT, supplied a modest institutional concession on the trade-finance interface, providing preferential access to developed-country markets for developing-country exports of manufactures and semi-manufactures.[^37]

The cumulative substantive content of the NIEO demands across these four domains can be characterized in synthetic terms. The project articulated a comprehensive program for the restructuring of international economic relations across the principal categories of commodities, technology, resources, and finance; the substantive ambition of the program was the structural transformation of the international economic order along the lines that the structuralist analytical framework had identified as necessary for developmental progress. The institutional outputs across the four domains registered substantial normative codification — the IPC and the Common Fund, the Set on Restrictive Business Practices, the elaboration of permanent sovereignty, the debt-relief resolution, the GSP — but substantially less material transformation than the substantive demands had sought. The gap between normative codification and material implementation, traced across the four domains, supplied the substantive content of the broader pattern that subchapter 8.8 now examines.

## 8.8 Rhetorical Victories and Material Defeats: The North–South Dialogue and Its Limits

The substantive outcomes of the NIEO project across the late 1970s registered the pattern that the report's interpretive framework has identified as central to the relationship between international organizations and decolonization: substantial normative codification combined with substantially constrained material implementation.

The principal institutional venue through which the substantive North–South dialogue was conducted across the late 1970s was the Conference on International Economic Cooperation (CIEC), commonly known as the Paris North–South Dialogue, which convened from December 1975 to June 1977. The CIEC was convened at French initiative as a forum bringing together developing and industrialized countries across four substantive commissions addressing energy, raw materials, development, and financial questions. The substantive significance of the CIEC operated through its institutional design: as a forum operating outside the formal UN architecture and with a limited and balanced membership, it offered the principal industrialized states a venue for substantive negotiation with developing-country representatives without the supermajority dynamics that conditioned General Assembly deliberations. The substantive outputs of the CIEC across its eighteen months of operation were modest; the conference closed in June 1977 without producing the comprehensive package agreement that the developing countries had sought, and its institutional legacy was principally negative — registering the substantive limits of the negotiating dynamics that the structural asymmetries of the late 1970s had generated.

The partial achievements of the NIEO project across the late 1970s and into the 1980s can be enumerated across several institutional outputs. The Generalized System of Preferences supplied a modest concession on preferential market access for developing-country exports of manufactures; the substantive content of this output operated through the unilateral GSP schemes implemented by the principal industrialized states across the 1970s and 1980s. The Common Fund for Commodities, agreed in 1980, supplied the institutional vehicle for the financing of commodity buffer stocks; the substantive content of this output, however, fell substantially short of the ambitious objectives of the original IPC: the negotiated capital structure of the Common Fund was substantially reduced from the initial proposals, and only a limited number of the commodity agreements envisaged by the IPC were actually concluded, with several of those failing to operate effectively. The debt-relief measures adopted by 18 developed-country donors following UNCTAD's 1978 resolution supplied modest bilateral concessions on official debt. The Set of Multilaterally Agreed Equitable Principles and Rules for the Control of Restrictive Business Practices, adopted in 1980 as a non-binding code, supplied the institutional articulation of competition principles for transnational corporate conduct; its non-binding character constrained its substantive operational effect.

The substantial demands that encountered resistance or were never operationalized constituted, in cumulative terms, the principal substantive content of the NIEO project. The Code of Conduct on the Transfer of Technology, negotiated for thirteen years (1972–1985) and ultimately abandoned, registered the substantive resistance of the principal industrialized states to the regulation of technology transfer along the lines that the developing countries had sought.[^37] The UN Code of Conduct on Transnational Corporations, negotiated across the late 1970s and 1980s, similarly failed to achieve binding adoption.[^49] The substantive reform of the international monetary system — including the proposal for an SDR-aid link, the restructuring of weighted voting at the IMF, and the broader transformation of the Bretton Woods institutions — was not accomplished across the period; the substantive governance of international monetary affairs remained under the weighted-voting architecture that the 1944 Bretton Woods settlement had inscribed. The substantive scale of development assistance from the industrialized countries fell substantially short of the 0.7 percent ODA target; the cumulative shortfall across the period registered the substantive resistance of the principal donor states to the scale of resource transfers that the NIEO demands had sought.[^50]

The structural constraints that conditioned the gap between rhetorical codification and material implementation, examined extensively in Chapter 7 in relation to the broader post-1960 transformation, deserve brief restatement in the specific context of the NIEO trajectory. The first constraint was the **weighted governance of the Bretton Woods institutions**: the substantive reforms of international monetary and financial relations that the NIEO project had demanded required the cooperation of the IMF and the World Bank, whose governance remained under the weighted-voting arrangements established in 1944 and through which the principal industrialized states retained controlling voting positions. The substantive consequence was that the institutional vehicles through which the NIEO demands would have to be operationalized were not the institutions in which the post-colonial states held majorities.

The second constraint was the **Security Council veto**, which conditioned the substantive enforcement of economic measures that the General Assembly might recommend. The substantive content of the NIEO project did not principally engage the Security Council architecture, but the broader pattern of structural protection of the principal industrialized states across the post-1945 institutional architecture operated as the persistent context within which the NIEO trajectory unfolded. The third constraint was the **financial dependence of UN operations** on assessed contributions from the principal industrialized states; the substantive operational capacities of UNCTAD, the specialized agencies, and the broader UN system depended on the continuing financial support of the states whose substantive positions on the NIEO demands were largely oppositional. The fourth constraint, less institutional than political-economic in character, was the **substantive market power** of the principal industrialized states across the principal domains of international economic relations. The substantive resistance of the principal industrialized states to the NIEO demands did not operate principally through institutional vetoes but through the substantive economic capacities — control over technology, finance, market access, intellectual property — that the institutional architecture of the post-1945 international economic order had consolidated.

The interpretive synthesis that emerges from this analysis can be articulated in the terms of the report's central paradox. The NIEO project accomplished substantial normative codification: the principles of the project entered the formal record of international law through the 1974 declarations and the Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States, and the substantive vocabulary of the project — permanent sovereignty over natural resources, non-reciprocity and differential treatment, sovereignty over economic activities, the regulation of transnational corporations — would condition the subsequent vocabulary of international economic relations across the decades. The project accomplished substantially less material transformation than its substantive demands had sought: the operational content of the demands across the principal domains of commodities, technology, resources, and finance was substantially constrained by the structural features of the international institutional architecture and the broader international political economy. The gap between rhetorical victory and material defeat was not a defect of the NIEO project's execution but a structural feature of the post-1945 international order — a feature whose persistence the project had identified, contested, and ultimately failed to displace.

## 8.9 The Reversal: Debt Crisis, Neoliberalism, and the Collapse of the NIEO Agenda

The trajectory by which the NIEO agenda was substantially reversed across the 1980s operated through the convergence of three principal dynamics whose interaction registered the broader transformation of the international political economy across the period: the Latin American debt crisis that began with the Mexican default of August 1982, the consolidation of neoliberal policy paradigms in the principal industrialized states under Reagan and Thatcher, and the progressive de-emphasis of policy negotiation within UNCTAD itself across the post-1987 period.

The Latin American debt crisis of 1982 supplied the immediate institutional dynamic through which the NIEO agenda was substantially reversed. The substantive accumulation of developing-country debt across the 1970s, conditioned by the recycling of petrodollars through commercial bank lending and by the substantial expansion of developing-country external borrowing, generated by the early 1980s a substantial debt burden whose servicing depended on the continuation of favorable terms in international financial markets. The substantive shift in those terms across 1979–1982 — operationalized through the substantial increase in U.S. interest rates following the Volcker shock of October 1979, the consequent appreciation of the U.S. dollar, and the substantial decline in commodity prices across the early 1980s — generated the conditions under which the Mexican default of August 1982 was followed by a cascading sequence of developing-country debt crises across Latin America, Africa, and parts of Asia. The substantive consequence for the NIEO project was the substantial recalibration of the bargaining position of developing countries within the international economic order: the substantive negotiating capacities that the cumulative diplomatic work of the post-1960 period had constructed were substantially eroded by the dependence of debt-crisis-affected developing countries on the IMF and World Bank for emergency financing, with the consequent acceptance of the conditionality arrangements that the structural adjustment programs of the 1980s operationalized.

The substantive content of the structural adjustment programs imposed across the developing world from the early 1980s onward registered the substantive inversion of the NIEO project's principal demands. Where the NIEO project had demanded the substantive transformation of international economic relations through the regulation of transnational corporations, the protection of permanent sovereignty over natural resources, the renegotiation of commodity prices, and the substantive expansion of resource transfers from industrialized to developing countries, the structural adjustment programs imposed by the IMF and the World Bank operated through the substantive opening of developing-country economies to international capital and trade, the reduction of state intervention in domestic economies, the privatization of state-owned enterprises, the contraction of public expenditure, and the broader alignment of developing-country economic policies with the substantive content of what would come to be called the Washington Consensus. The substantive significance of this inversion lay in the substantive reversal of the analytical framework through which international economic relations were understood. The structuralist analytical framework that had supplied the intellectual foundations of the NIEO project was substantively displaced across the 1980s by the neoclassical framework that the Washington Consensus operationalized; the substantive content of international economic governance migrated from the language of structural transformation to the language of market liberalization, registering the broader paradigm shift that the period operationalized.

The consolidation of neoliberal policy paradigms in the principal industrialized states under Ronald Reagan in the United States (from January 1981) and Margaret Thatcher in the United Kingdom (from May 1979) supplied the political context within which the substantive reversal operated. The substantive content of the Reagan–Thatcher policy frameworks — operationalized through tax cuts, deregulation, the contraction of public expenditure, the confrontation with organized labor, and the broader retreat from the Keynesian and developmental policy frameworks of the post-1945 period — registered the substantive shift in the political center of gravity within the principal industrialized states.
