The 1963 expansion of the Council's non-permanent membership from six to ten, accomplished through the amendment to Article 23 examined in Chapter 7, addressed the substantive demographic transformation of the broader UN through the recomposed elected membership but left intact the principal structural protection of the great powers. The substantive consequence has been the persistence across the post-1945 period of an institutional architecture in which the binding enforcement mechanisms of the UN system are subject to the substantive consent of states whose composition was calibrated to the political configuration of 1945. The substantive content of contemporary debates over Security Council reform — including the substantive proposals for the expansion of permanent membership to include states from Africa, Latin America, and Asia, and the parallel proposals for the modification or limitation of the veto — registers the continuing salience of the structural inequality that the 1945 settlement inscribed.

The second such legacy operates through the durability of weighted governance in the Bretton Woods institutions. As Chapter 7 examined, the substantive consequence of the post-1944 architecture of the IMF and the World Bank was the persistence across the post-1960 period of an institutional architecture in which voting rights were allocated according to financial contributions, with the result that the principal industrialized states retained controlling voting positions even as the membership of the institutions expanded to include the post-colonial states. The substantive content of subsequent reform efforts across the 1980s, 1990s, and 2000s has modified the specific distribution of voting rights — with substantive concessions to the increased economic weight of emerging-economy states including the People's Republic of China — but has not displaced the underlying weighted-governance principle. The substantive consequence for the broader institutional architecture of global economic governance operates through the persistent asymmetry between the universal-membership bodies of the UN proper (where post-colonial states retain demographic majorities) and the weighted-governance financial institutions (where the principal industrialized states retain controlling positions). The substantive demands of the NIEO project, examined in Chapter 8, identified this asymmetry as a principal structural feature of the international order whose transformation the project sought; the substantive defeat of the project across the 1980s registered the durability of the asymmetry, which has continued to operate as a defining feature of the contemporary international order.

The third such legacy operates through the persistent gap between G77 and OECD voting positions documented in the political-science literature. As Chapter 7 noted in its synthetic assessment, "the gap in UN voting positions between G77 states and members of the OECD was just as large in 2018 as it was in 1980." The substantive significance of this finding for the present chapter's argument operates through its registration of the durability of the North–South cleavage that the post-1960 transformation had generated, even across the post-NIEO decades during which the substantive ambitions of the Third World project had been substantially reversed. The cleavage operates not as a transient feature of particular political moments but as a structural feature of the contemporary international order whose persistence registers the unresolved character of the substantive issues that the post-1960 diplomacy had identified.

The fourth such legacy operates through the recurring patterns of financial dependence that condition the operational reach of multilateral institutions. The substantive content of this dependence, examined across the three operational domains of Chapter 9, operates through the architecture of assessed contributions for peacekeeping (in which the principal industrialized states bear a disproportionate share of total funding), the architecture of voluntary contributions for development assistance (in which the substantive scale of multilateral flows depends on the policy priorities of the principal donor states), and the regular-budget assessments for the broader UN system (in which the financial sustainability of the organization depends on the continuing cooperation of the principal contributors). The substantive consequence for the operational reach of the multilateral architecture is that the substantive demographic majorities of the UN are conditioned, in their operational implementation, by the financial capacities that the principal industrialized states retain. The pattern has operated as a recurring constraint across the post-1960 period and continues to condition the contemporary operation of the multilateral system.

The synthetic assessment of these four structural legacies can be articulated in the following terms. The contemporary international order is not the institutional architecture that the 1945 settlement constructed, nor is it the institutional architecture that the post-1960 anti-colonial transformation would have produced had its ambitions been fully implemented. It is the institutional architecture that the unfinished encounter between these two trajectories has yielded: the principal structural features of the 1945 settlement — the Security Council veto, the weighted governance of the Bretton Woods institutions, the financial dependence of multilateral operations — persist as durable features of the contemporary order; the principal achievements of the post-1960 transformation — the demographic expansion of the General Assembly, the substantive vocabulary of self-determination and economic justice, the operational architecture of peacekeeping, development cooperation, and human rights monitoring — operate alongside them as substantive but constrained features of the same architecture. The substantive content of contemporary international politics operates through the continuing interaction of these two layers of the institutional inheritance.

The interpretive significance of identifying these structural legacies as active features of the contemporary order rather than as residual artifacts of a past institutional moment is substantial. The substantive content of contemporary debates over global governance reform — including the reform of the Security Council, the reform of international financial governance, the reform of international trade and investment regimes, the architecture of global responses to pandemic and climate challenges — operates through the institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies that the twentieth-century trajectory had constructed. The structural features that condition the operational reach of contemporary multilateral cooperation are not external constraints imposed on a neutral institutional architecture; they are the constitutive features of an architecture whose substantive character was substantially shaped by the entangled relationship between international organizations and decolonization that the report has reconstructed.

## 10.4 Unfinished Agendas of Self-Determination

The fourth principal interpretive yield of the historical reconstruction concerns the unfinished agendas of self-determination that the twentieth-century trajectory bequeathed to the contemporary period. The substantive content of these agendas operates across multiple dimensions whose synthetic articulation supplies one of the principal connections between the report's historical analysis and the contemporary international politics that it is intended to illuminate.

The first dimension operates through the persistence of the remaining non-self-governing territories on the UN list. As Chapter 5 noted, the General Assembly's 1946 listing of seventy-two non-self-governing territories under Resolution 66(I) supplied the initial inventory of the formal category established by Chapter XI of the Charter; the subsequent decolonizations across the post-1960 period progressively reduced the list to the seventeen territories that remain inscribed as of the contemporary period. The substantive content of the Special Committee on Decolonization's ongoing work, examined in Chapter 6, continues to engage these territories — including Gibraltar, the Falkland Islands (Malvinas), New Caledonia, French Polynesia, Western Sahara, and others — through the institutional vehicles that the post-1960 architecture had constructed. The persistence of these cases on the UN's formal agenda across decades after the principal wave of decolonization had completed registers the structural limits of the post-1960 framework: the categorical inversion accomplished by Resolution 1514 did not produce the immediate and unconditional end of colonial rule that its textual provisions had required, and the substantive content of decolonization in the remaining cases continues to operate through the institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies that the post-1960 transformation had elaborated.

The second dimension operates through the contested decolonization cases whose unresolved character registers the structural limits of the post-1960 framework. The Western Sahara dispute, examined in Chapter 6, exemplifies the operational limits of the Resolution 1514 framework when applied to situations in which the determination of the relevant "self" and the procedures for the expression of that self's will are themselves contested. The 1975 International Court of Justice Advisory Opinion on Western Sahara applied the framework of Resolutions 1514 and 1541 to the specific situation of the Spanish Sahara, finding that the ties between the territory and Morocco and Mauritania did not preclude the application of the principle of self-determination. The subsequent decades of contested administration — through the Moroccan annexation and the continuing claim of the Sahrawi Arab Democratic Republic — have demonstrated that the normative framework of the Declaration cannot by itself resolve disputes in which the substantive determinations of self and procedure are contested. The Chagos Archipelago case, examined in the 2019 ICJ Advisory Opinion noted in Chapter 6, supplies a parallel instance in which the substantive framework of Resolution 1514 has been deployed to address decolonization obligations whose substantive content had not been satisfied by the unilateral arrangements through which territories had been separated from their former colonial associations. The Palestinian case, whose institutional pre-history extended through the British Mandate examined in Chapter 2 and whose substantive content has structured UN engagement across the entire post-1945 period through UNTSO, UNDOF, UNIFIL, and the broader UN architecture, registers perhaps the most enduring instance of unresolved decolonization on the international agenda. The New Caledonia case, with the referendums of 2018, 2020, and 2021 conducted within the framework that Resolution 1541 had codified, registers the continuing operational relevance of the post-1960 architecture across the contemporary period.

The third dimension operates through the tensions between self-determination and territorial integrity that Article 6 of Resolution 1514 inscribed and whose subsequent applications have generated continuing jurisprudential and political contestation. As Chapter 6 examined in detail, the textual incompatibility between Article 2's affirmation of self-determination and Article 6's safeguard of territorial integrity was accommodated through the principle of *uti possidetis juris* — the inheritance of colonial-era borders by newly independent states — which the academic literature characterizes as the principal interpretive resolution of the Declaration's internal tension. The substantive content of this resolution operated through the limitation of self-determination to "colonial communities with established borders" and the foreclosure of subsequent secessionist invocations of self-determination by sub-state groups. The contested applications of this framework across the post-1989 period — including the substantive disputes over Kosovo, Crimea, the broader category of contemporary secessionist claims, and the parallel questions of internal self-determination and the rights of indigenous peoples — have generated continuing jurisprudential and political contestation whose substantive content operates through the institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies that the post-1960 architecture had elaborated. The principle of self-determination, codified through Resolution 1514 in its colonial paradigm, continues to operate as a substantive principle of international law whose extension beyond the colonial paradigm remains the substantive matter at issue in contemporary contests.

The fourth dimension operates through the broader unfinished business of substantive decolonization that the political codification of self-determination did not exhaust. The substantive content of decolonization extends, as Chapter 1 articulated, beyond the discrete legal event of formal sovereignty transfer to encompass the construction of new state institutions, the renegotiation of economic relations, and the contestation of inherited cultural and intellectual hierarchies. The economic dimension of this unfinished agenda was substantively articulated through the NIEO project examined in Chapter 8; the substantive defeat of the project across the 1980s did not displace the underlying analytical claim that formal political independence does not exhaust the requirements of substantive decolonization but registered the structural constraints within which the substantive transformation would have to be conducted. The cultural and epistemic dimensions of the unfinished agenda — including the substantive content of post-colonial intellectual and cultural critique, the institutional reconstruction of educational and research capacities in post-colonial states, the broader pattern of contestation over inherited knowledge hierarchies — operate substantially outside the institutional terrain that the report has examined but constitute substantive dimensions of the broader trajectory whose continuing salience registers the unfinished character of the substantive decolonization that the political achievements of the post-1960 era had only begun to accomplish.

The synthetic interpretation that emerges from these four dimensions can be articulated in the terms of the report's broader argument. The substantive content of self-determination as a foundational principle of post-1960 international law was substantively achieved through the institutional vehicles examined in the preceding chapters; the substantive completion of the trajectory that the principle had inaugurated has not been accomplished. The unfinished agendas of self-determination operate not as residual elements of a past institutional moment but as constitutive features of the contemporary international order whose continuing salience registers the durability of both the achievements and the limits of the twentieth-century trajectory. The questions raised by the Mandates System examined in Chapter 2 and elaborated through Resolution 1514 examined in Chapter 6 continue to structure the international politics of the present in ways that the report's historical reconstruction has been designed to illuminate.

## 10.5 From Mandates to NIEO: The Enduring Relevance of Twentieth-Century Questions

The fifth principal interpretive yield of the historical reconstruction concerns the enduring analytical relevance of the questions raised by both the Mandates System and the NIEO project for understanding twenty-first century international relations. The substantive content of contemporary debates across multiple domains operates through conceptual vocabularies and institutional architectures that the twentieth-century trajectory had elaborated; the analytical categories developed through the report supply durable interpretive resources for contemporary scholarship and policy debate.

The substantive continuity between the Mandates System's question of international supervision over governance and contemporary debates over international engagement with state conduct operates across multiple institutional domains. The substantive content of contemporary peacekeeping in contested-consent environments, examined in Chapter 9 with reference to recent mission closures including MINUSMA in Mali, operates through the institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies that the Suez and ONUC experiences had elaborated. The substantive content of contemporary international engagement with fragile and conflict-affected states — through development cooperation, security sector reform, electoral assistance, and the broader architecture of state-building — operates through institutional logics whose substantive content the trajectory from the League's Mandates Section to UNDP and the specialized agencies had constructed. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the responsibility to protect and the broader category of humanitarian intervention operates through the tensions between sovereignty and international supervisory responsibility that the Mandates System had first institutionalized. The contemporary question of the appropriate relationship between international supervision and host-state sovereignty is, in substantive terms, the same question that the Permanent Mandates Commission had first encountered in its supervisory work; the institutional vehicles through which the question is engaged have been substantially transformed, but the substantive content of the question registers the durability of the analytical issues that the twentieth-century trajectory had identified.

The substantive continuity between the NIEO project's questions of economic justice and contemporary debates over the international economic order operates with comparable clarity. The substantive content of contemporary debates over sovereignty over natural resources — operating through the institutional vehicles of investment treaty disputes, the negotiation of mining and hydrocarbon concessions, the substantive content of permanent sovereignty as elaborated through subsequent jurisprudence — registers the continuing salience of the principle that Resolution 1803 of 1962 and the 1974 declarations had codified. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the regulation of transnational corporations — operating through the work of the UN Working Group on Business and Human Rights, the negotiation of the proposed UN binding treaty on business and human rights, the broader corpus of voluntary guidelines and reporting frameworks — registers the unfinished agenda of the UNCTAD Code of Conduct on Restrictive Business Practices and the parallel UN Code of Conduct on Transnational Corporations that the NIEO project had sought. The substantive content of contemporary debates over climate justice and common-but-differentiated responsibilities — operating through the institutional vehicles of the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement — registers the continuing operation of the analytical framework that the NIEO project had elaborated, with the substantive principle of differentiated obligations as between industrialized and developing countries operating through conceptual categories that the post-1960 transformation had codified. The substantive content of contemporary debates over debt and development finance — operating through the institutional vehicles of the IMF, the World Bank, the regional development banks, and the broader architecture of international financial governance — registers the unfinished agenda of debt relief and the broader reform of international financial governance that the NIEO project had sought. The substantive content of contemporary debates over technology transfer — operating through the institutional vehicles of the TRIPS Agreement and its subsequent elaboration, the negotiations over access to medicines and to climate-relevant technologies, the broader category of intellectual property and development — registers the unfinished agenda of the technology code that UNCTAD had negotiated between 1972 and 1985.

The substantive continuity between the broader institutional questions of the twentieth-century trajectory and contemporary debates over the reform of global governance institutions operates across multiple substantive domains. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the reform of the Security Council operates through the institutional architecture that the 1945 settlement had constructed and that the 1963 amendment had partially modified. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the reform of the international financial institutions operates through the weighted-governance architecture that the 1944 Bretton Woods settlement had inscribed. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the architecture of the multilateral trading system operates through the substantive vocabulary of non-reciprocity and differential treatment that the UNCTAD work had elaborated. The substantive content of contemporary debates over the broader architecture of multilateral cooperation operates through the institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies that the entangled relationship between international organizations and decolonization had constructed.

The analytical categories developed through the report supply, in the synthetic terms that the preceding subchapters have articulated, durable interpretive resources for contemporary scholarship and policy debate. The distinction between guardianship and contestation, articulated in Chapter 1 and elaborated through the analysis of each substantive chapter, supplies an analytical framework that contemporary scholarship can deploy in its assessment of contemporary international institutions whose substantive character operates through the simultaneous dual functions that the twentieth-century trajectory had inscribed. The distinction between normative codification and material implementation, articulated in Chapter 1 and elaborated through the analysis across the substantive chapters, supplies an analytical framework that contemporary policy debate can deploy in its assessment of the substantive content of contemporary normative achievements. The distinction between universal-membership and weighted-governance institutions, articulated in Chapter 7 and elaborated through the analysis in Chapter 8, supplies an analytical framework that contemporary debates over global governance reform can deploy in their assessment of the institutional architecture within which substantive reform would have to be conducted. The distinction between political decolonization and substantive economic transformation, articulated through the analysis in Chapter 8, supplies an analytical framework that contemporary debates over development, equity, and the structure of international economic relations can deploy in their assessment of the unfinished character of the substantive transformation that the political codifications had not, by themselves, exhausted.

The substantive significance of these analytical continuities is that the report's historical reconstruction is not merely a backward-looking exercise in institutional history but an analytical contribution to the understanding of the contemporary international order whose substantive character was substantially shaped by the entangled trajectory that the report has examined. The questions raised by the Mandates System and the NIEO are not antiquarian curiosities but the substantive analytical resources through which the contemporary international order can be understood.

## 10.6 Toward a Balanced Historiographical Synthesis

The concluding section of the report articulates the synthetic interpretive contribution that the historical reconstruction has yielded and its position within the broader historiographical landscape. The four interpretive commitments articulated in Chapter 1 — the refusal of teleology, the insistence on the agency of colonized actors, the treatment of institutional and ideational as mutually constitutive, and the consistent distinction between normative and material achievements — have been substantiated across the cumulative analysis of the preceding chapters in ways that can now be brought into synthetic focus.

The first commitment was the refusal of teleology in both its progressive and its declinist variants. The cumulative analysis across the substantive chapters has demonstrated that the trajectory from 1919 to the late twentieth century was neither the predetermined unfolding of a universal norm of self-determination nor a closed circuit in which imperial domination reproduced itself in ever more sophisticated forms. The contingency of the wartime negotiations examined in Chapter 4, the contested character of the institutional politics of the Fourth Committee and the CINSGT examined in Chapter 5, the political conjuncture of 1960 that produced Resolution 1514 examined in Chapter 6, the substantive achievements and substantive defeats of the NIEO project examined in Chapter 8, and the institutional innovations and structural constraints of the new operational functions examined in Chapter 9 all register the contingent and contested character of the trajectory. The substantive outcomes of the trajectory were repeatedly open, repeatedly contested, and repeatedly conditioned by the substantive diplomatic and political agency of multiple actors. The refusal of teleology has supplied the analytical resources to acknowledge both the genuine achievements and the substantive limits of the trajectory without conflating either with an inevitable historical logic.

The second commitment was the insistence on the agency of colonized actors as participants in, not merely objects of, international politics. The cumulative analysis across the substantive chapters has demonstrated this agency across multiple institutional terrains. The petitioners to the Permanent Mandates Commission examined in Chapter 2, the participants in the Brussels Congress of February 1927 examined in Chapter 3, the post-colonial states that constructed the post-1955 institutional coalitions examined in Chapters 5, 6, and 7, the architects of the Non-Aligned Movement and the Group of 77 examined in Chapter 8, the substantive participants in the elaboration of human rights instruments and the anti-apartheid campaign examined in Chapter 9 — all register the substantive agency of colonized and post-colonial actors as constitutive participants in the construction of the twentieth-century international order. The substantive content of the institutional vehicles examined across the chapters was shaped, in substantial part, by the diplomatic agency that these actors exercised. The post-1960 transformation of the UN, examined in Chapter 7, was not an institutional accommodation that the principal industrialized states conceded to a passive post-colonial constituency; it was the substantive product of post-colonial diplomatic agency conducted through the institutional vehicles that the post-1945 architecture had supplied. The substantive achievements of Resolutions 1514 and 1541 examined in Chapter 6, the substantive elaboration of the NIEO project examined in Chapter 8, and the substantive content of the new operational functions examined in Chapter 9 were the products of substantive diplomatic agency whose recognition supplies one of the principal historiographical contributions of the report.

The third commitment was the treatment of the institutional and ideational as mutually constitutive. The cumulative analysis across the substantive chapters has demonstrated this mutual constitution at every principal institutional moment. The "sacred trust of civilization" formula examined in Chapter 2 supplied the conceptual infrastructure within which the institutional procedures of the Mandates System operated; the institutional procedures, in turn, generated the documentary record and the petition practice that elaborated the substantive content of the formula. The Atlantic Charter language examined in Chapter 4 supplied the conceptual infrastructure within which the institutional architecture of the Charter settlement was constructed; the institutional architecture, in turn, supplied the procedural and substantive vehicles through which the conceptual content was progressively elaborated. The substantive content of permanent sovereignty over natural resources examined in Chapter 7 supplied the conceptual infrastructure within which the institutional work of UNCTAD and the G77 was conducted; the institutional work, in turn, supplied the substantive material through which the conceptual content was progressively codified. The pattern operates with comparable clarity across each principal institutional moment examined in the report. The substantive consequence of this mutual constitution is that neither the institutional nor the ideational can be analyzed in isolation from the other; the substantive content of each was conditioned by the other through the recursive pattern that the report has traced.

The fourth commitment was the consistent distinction between normative and material achievements. The cumulative analysis across the substantive chapters has demonstrated this distinction with particular clarity at each principal institutional moment, as the preceding subchapters have systematized. The substantive consequence of consistently maintaining this distinction has been the recovery of a more historically accurate account of the twentieth-century trajectory than either the celebratory or the dismissive readings can supply.

The report's contribution to the third historiographical moment characterized in Chapter 1 can now be articulated in synthetic terms. The third moment, as Chapter 1 characterized it, has "moved beyond the opposition" between the earlier institutionalist reading of progressive normative achievement and the subsequent critical reading of imperial complicity, "recovering the agency of colonized actors within international institutions, tracing transnational anti-colonial networks across the official–unofficial divide, and exploring the unintended consequences of imperial designs." The report's substantive contribution to this third moment operates through three principal dimensions. First, it has recovered the substantive diplomatic and institutional agency of post-colonial actors as constitutive participants in the construction of the twentieth-century international order, demonstrating that the substantive achievements of the post-1960 transformation were the products of sustained diplomatic agency conducted across decades rather than concessions granted by an accommodating great-power consensus. Second, it has traced the transnational networks across the official–unofficial divide, demonstrating that the interwar counter-mobilizational tradition of the League Against Imperialism examined in Chapter 3 operated in implicit dialogue with the official supervisory tradition of the Permanent Mandates Commission examined in Chapter 2, and that the post-1945 institutional architecture absorbed and transformed elements of both traditions across the subsequent decades. Third, it has explored the unintended consequences of imperial designs at multiple institutional moments — the unintended internationalization of colonial affairs through the petition procedures of the Mandates System, the unintended radicalization of self-determination through the cumulative anti-colonial appropriation of the Article 73(e) reporting regime, the unintended consolidation of an anti-colonial majority through the institutional architecture of one-state-one-vote that the 1945 settlement had inscribed — demonstrating that the substantive trajectory of the twentieth-century international order was substantially shaped by consequences that the principal architects of its institutional moments had not anticipated.

The substantive interpretive position that emerges from this historiographical synthesis can be articulated in the terms with which the report opened. The relationship between international organizations and decolonization across the twentieth century was neither the liberation that the most ambitious anti-colonial projections of the post-1960 era had anticipated nor the unbroken continuity of imperial domination that the most pessimistic critical readings have identified. It was a complex, contested, and unfinished restructuring of world order whose substantive achievements were genuine, whose substantive limits were structural, and whose substantive legacies — both emancipatory and constraining — continue to define the international politics of the present.

The substantive achievements of the trajectory operate through multiple dimensions whose synthetic articulation supplies the cumulative interpretive yield of the report. The institutional infrastructure of international supervision over colonial governance, inaugurated through the Permanent Mandates Commission and elaborated through the post-1945 Trusteeship System, established the principle that the conduct of states toward dependent peoples was a matter of legitimate international concern. The categorical codification of self-determination through Resolution 1514, supplemented by the procedural specification of Resolution 1541, transformed the substantive vocabulary of international politics by inscribing the right of all peoples to determine their political status as a foundational principle of post-1960 international law. The institutional vehicles of post-colonial diplomacy — the Non-Aligned Movement, the Group of 77, UNCTAD, the broader chapter architecture across the specialized agencies — supplied the substantive coordination capacities through which post-colonial states could articulate substantive demands within the multilateral architecture. The substantive vocabulary of permanent sovereignty over natural resources, non-reciprocity and differential treatment in international trade, common but differentiated responsibilities, and the broader corpus of international development law constructed the conceptual infrastructure within which contemporary debates over global equity and justice continue to be conducted. The institutional infrastructure of human rights instruments, codified across the post-1948 trajectory and substantively shaped by the anti-colonial and anti-discrimination agenda, established the substantive framework within which contemporary engagement with state conduct toward individuals and groups operates. The operational architecture of peacekeeping, development cooperation, and human rights monitoring, elaborated through the cumulative response to the demands that decolonization had generated, transformed the operational character of the UN system from the cautious deliberative organization that the 1945 settlement had constructed into the operational system that contemporary international politics depends upon.

The substantive limits of the trajectory operate through the structural features whose persistence the report has systematically traced. The Security Council veto and the four-of-five permanent members' colonial origins; the weighted governance of the Bretton Woods institutions; the financial dependence of multilateral operations on assessed contributions from the principal industrialized states; the persistent asymmetry between universal-membership UN bodies and weighted-governance financial institutions; the structural defeats of the NIEO project that registered the broader transformation of the international political economy across the 1980s; the unfinished agendas of self-determination registered in the remaining non-self-governing territories and the contested decolonization cases — all operate as constitutive features of the contemporary international order whose continuing operation conditions the politics of the present.

The substantive legacy of the trajectory operates through the durable institutional and conceptual infrastructure that the twentieth-century encounter between international organizations and decolonization had constructed. The contemporary international order operates, in its substantive features, through institutional vehicles and conceptual vocabularies whose substantive character was substantially shaped by the entangled relationship that this report has examined. The unfinished business of substantive decolonization — political, economic, and cultural — operates as the substantive content of contemporary international politics whose continuing salience registers the durability of the questions that the twentieth-century trajectory had identified, contested, and only partially resolved.

The report closes with the substantive reaffirmation of the interpretive judgment with which it opened. International organizations and decolonization mutually constituted one another across the twentieth century. The institutions of international governance were not simply repurposed by decolonization, nor did they simply absorb and contain it; they were transformed by it, even as they shaped the forms in which it could be pursued. The result was neither liberation nor unbroken domination but a complex, contested, and unfinished restructuring of world order — one whose legacies, both emancipatory and constraining, continue to define the international politics of the present. The substantive task of contemporary scholarship and policy debate is not the celebration or the dismissal of this restructuring but the substantive engagement with the unfinished agendas that it bequeathed and with the structural features whose persistence continues to condition the substantive possibilities of contemporary international cooperation. The historical reconstruction that the report has conducted is offered as an analytical contribution to that substantive engagement.
